Midyear core inflation drop opens the door for September Fed ease

● Inflation rotation ahead: Global goods pressure builds, services pressure cools

0 Looking for a lift in consumer spending as lower inflation boosts real incomes

• Next week: Soft China data; US RS control and IP gain; ECB guides Sept. ease

## How fast, how far?

A second consecutive low US core CPI print was the main event this week, delivering enough “more good data” to open the door for a September Fed ease. The moderation of core inflation into midyear should, however, be seen as a synchronized global development. Globe core CPI (ex. China and Türkiye) is tracking a 0.15% increase last month, an outcome that pushes the three-month rate down to a three-year low of 2.7%ar. Constructive inflation news is also laying the ground for Western Europe central banks (ECB, BoE, Riksbank) to join the Fed in lowering rates before the end of the quarter. With a number of EM central banks already cutting, a long-awaited global easing cycle is set to begin.

The central question ahead relates to the speed and magnitude of this easing. Our top-down view that the cycle will be slow and shallow contrasts with market pricing of more than a 150bp fall in rates for the DM ex. Japan through the end of next year. This view is based on two judgments: 1) the coming slide in core inflation will prove incomplete, and 2) the US and global expansions will remain on solid ground. There are risks worth considering on both fronts, and we recognize uncertainties related to the political backdrop. But the immediate challenge is to our sticky inflation view. Here we have cautioned against the extrapolation of

short-term inflation swings that have a high noise-to-signal ratio. The first half has delivered a sharp up-and-down swing concentrated in core services prices (Figure 1). Looking through this volatility, US and global core CPI inflation are both tracking above a 3%ar gain in 1H24, an outcome in line with 2H23 results and our forecast at the start of the year.

We caution against extrapolating the recent momentum downshift but also recognize the significance in validating the transitory nature of the early year rise. This validation is providing central bankers the confidence boost to move forward on their easing guidance. How far they ultimately move will depend, in part, on the interplay of two recent macroeconomic developments shaping 2H24 inflation performance.

Building goods pressure as services inflation moderates. Last year’s global core inflation slide largely reflected the normalization in global supply chains and unwind of commodity shocks. With support from a weak global manufacturing sector, global goods prices stabilized late last year. Our forecast anticipates a moderation in service sector inflation and goods price stability. While the latest deceleration in service price inflation has been surprisingly large, goods price inflation is now shifting upwards. Spiking transportation costs and a recovery in global nontech production point to a roughly 1%ar jump over coming months, underscored by the latest PMIs (Figure 2).

Figure 3: Nominal wage growth
![](images/9481f07496d299512ba78ef3d04d0f412b19a7bad98a98810c62bdcc88e4d508.jpg)

Absolute and relative easing in US labor markets. Tight labor markets and elevated wage inflation are key elements underlying our sticky inflation forecast. While these conditions remain in place across most of the world, US labor market pressures have eased more rapidly than expected. Despite sustained strong employment gains, the US unemployment rate is up 0.5%-pt over the past year and a clearly established downtrend in wage inflation is now in place (Figure 3). Except for EM Asia, labor cost inflation remains close to cyclical highs—most notably in the Euro area where wage gains continue to rise at a close to 5%ar. These factors reinforce upside risks to both growth and inflation.

While their impact on the path of global core inflation is not clear, these two developments will likely reinforce the nearterm case for central bank easing. Notably, better news from the more inertial services price basket will likely be given more weight than any disappointment related to the more volatile goods basket. At the same time, a growing bias toward better relative outcomes in the US will support Fed easing. While domestic drivers are the primary guide for policy-setting everywhere, the important role of the Fed easing in currency markets and broader global financial conditions will promote cutting elsewhere as well.

## Consumers benefit from lower inflation

Headline inflation is falling even more sharply than core into midyear on the back of a drop in energy prices. We have expected this decline to support consumer spending gains, with a boost to retail sales, which slipped in recent months. Our global retail sales tracker for May sends a positive signal alongside gains in global June auto sales. But continued weakness in consumer confidence is a reminder of the headwinds still facing household spending (Figure 4). Next week brings our first snapshot of June retail spending; we look for a modest gain in US control and a soft reading from China.

Figure 4: Consumer confidence and spending, global ex China
![](images/7b9957bf6fb2e9a03c41aa18d4cf315ff824e52e745f427af72c4e0756ac9b14.jpg)

## Rising incomes support BoJ July hike

Incoming data suggest that Japanese domestic consumption, which had been sluggish for a year since 2Q23, is finally breaking away from a year-long slump. Consumers look to be perking up at midyear in response to rising wage momentum (with full-time scheduled earnings advancing at a decadeshigh pace) and the government’s one-off tax cuts. This week, SME business sentiment also turned up on household-related businesses. We now see some upside risks to our GDP-based consumption for quarters ahead and are comfortable with our out-of-consensus call for the BoJ to hike later this month.

## Swiftly, swiftly flew the ship

The path for broad easing in Western Europe looks to be opening, but the risks surrounding the timing of next moves vary across the region.

The ECB is almost certain to stay on hold next week while describing incoming data as “broadly confirming” its inflation outlook. With staff inflation projections incorporating market expectations of further cuts this language would signal it is on track for a September ease. The risk associated with elevated wage gains and service price inflation persists but has been tempered by recent downshifts in inflation momentum and the PMI.

We expect the BoE to ease next month, but upside risks to 2Q24 GDP growth and hawkish comments from Chief Economist Pill temper our confidence. Next week, we look for support from a decline in June core CPI to 3.4%oya. But there is upside risk to this forecast from last month’s Taylor Swift concerts, which had a large impact on Swedish inflation for May.

Sweden’s June CPI showed a partial reversal of the Swiftflation and core inflation now looks likely to hit 2%oya in the coming months. In response to this downside surprise, we have updated our Riksbank forecast to include cuts in both August and September.

## China: Diminished expectation

Next week China releases significant data—the 2Q24 GDP report and major June activity indicators—and holds its 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum. The news is not likely to soothe concerns around growth. We expect the data to highlight an imbalanced economy dependent on external demand for an industrial sector with excess capacity. This week’s trade report showed June exports rising solidly and we expect IP to be up 0.5%m/m in next week’s report. By contrast, we expect next week’s data to show consumer spending remaining sluggish and FAI weak as public sector manufacturing investments partially offset private sector and real estate weakness.

China’s macroeconomic imbalances are underscored by ongoing weakness in June pricing, which points to profit pressures on private sector businesses. Next week’s important 3rd Plenum will discuss the structural reform agenda for the next five years. We do not expect the meeting to deliver any major policy surprises. Later this month, the cyclical policy discussion at the Politburo meeting will focus more on the 2H outlook. Here too, we do not expect the rollout of any large-scale stimulus, but rather a continuation of the moderate growthsupportive policy mode.

## Fed helps, but EM policy made at home

The shifting of expectations around the start of the Fed easing cycle is likely to reverberate through the EM, providing a green light for earlier cuts. However, it would be a mistake to put too much weight on the influence. Domestic conditions play the key role in policy setting for nearly all central banks, including those in the EM:

Inflation improving in EMEA EM: A potentially earlier Fed start is likely to only have only a residual impact on CEE rate-setting via FX moves given net negative foreign financing needs and gross needs primarily from the EU. Nevertheless, earlier sticky inflation looks to finally be moderating even if to still-elevated levels. Much of this owes to a common downtrend in goods pricing, but June inflation reports this week surprised to the downside in Poland, Czechia, and Romania. We expect the CNB to deliver four 25bp cuts this year, but risks are skewing toward more. In Hungary, where inflation remains sticky and was a standout this week with an upside surprise for June, the Fed spillover is helping pave the way for what we expected to be three 25bp cuts this year. By contrast, the NBP’s governor has guided adamantly to no cuts until 2026 (we expect a first cut in 2Q25).

Growth and leverage central in EM Asia: A Fed ease in September would clear the decks for several EM Asian central banks to ease in 4Q, including in India, Indonesia, Korea, and Philippines. But with lower policy rates already, the bar for easing is higher and primarily influenced by domestic considerations. The Bank of Korea kept rates on hold this week and pushed back against expectations of near-term easing, which markets had been anticipating, noting household leverage risks could make any easing cycle likely to be a shallow one. We look for this to begin in October, after the Fed. Similarly, in India, resurgent food prices, which pushed headline June CPI above 5%oya this week, and financial stability concerns are likely to keep the RBI cautious at the August review. We expect the RBI, like the BoK, to begin cutting in 4Q, something not reflected in more hawkish market pricing.

Political risk premia in the mix for Latam: Political uncertainties are dominating market moves in Brazil and Mexico. Indeed, inflation in Brazil has declined and is roughly in line with the BCB’s 3% target. Rather, volatility around fiscal targets and threats to central bank independence placed a risk premium in policy rates. While markets see rate hikes, we think the BCB is on hold through 2025. In Mexico, a benign core inflation reading for June, along with a dovish set of Banxico minutes, leads us to expect a 25bp rate cut in August. However, both domestic and US political uncertainties will likely temper the pace of the easing cycle.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 2Q24 global real GDP growth ticked up 0.1%-pt this week to 2.3%ar (Fig. 1), while the top-down global nowcaster for 2Q similarly edged up to 2.4%ar. The nowcaster is tracking 3Q growth at 2.3%ar (Table 2).

Half of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in a little over half of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests an acceleration in capex growth from 2.4%ar in 1Q24 to 3.0%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

Global (ex China) consumer confidence moved sideways in June, avoiding a third monthly contraction but remaining at a depressed level. Global auto sales, however, were more encouraging, up 2% in June with broad-based gains offsetting US weakness. On the business side, global capex looks to have posted a solid expansion in 2Q.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds rallied over the past week, mainly on the back of soft US inflation data. In the US, we now forecast the first Fed ease in September (vs. November previously) with a quarterly pace of cuts thereafter. We lower our YE24 2- and 10-year forecasts to 4.30% and 4.15% to reflect an earlier onset to easing. Pre-easing cyclicals also favor further long-end steepening, and the carry profile is better than outright longs, so we maintain 5s/30s steepeners. However, the curve locally appears too steep after controlling for front-end yield levels and may turn more rangebound in the coming weeks, so we recommend adding beta-weighted shorts in 2Y Treasuries.

In the Euro area, ongoing political/fiscal uncertainty will likely keep French spreads in a 60-70bp range over Bunds vs. \~45bp before the snap elections. We hold tactical longs in 5Y Germany and steepeners in 2Yx2Y/10Yx10Y EUR swaps, take profit long 1Yx1Y ESTR and 10s/15s Germany steepeners and keep a bias for a steeper 5s/30s German curve. In the UK, we expect a 25bp cut from the BoE in August, but it is a close call. With markets pricing in two cuts, the Aug/Nov24 MPC OIS curve will struggle to flatten further and we remove our flattening bias. We stay long 5Yx5Y SONIA vs. SOFR.

In EM, following a large upside surprise in 1H24 in EM growth, 2H is set to see a slowdown and a more differentiated progress on disinflation. As a result, we see more value in idiosyncratic EM carry than clear market direction. With the inclusion of India into the GBI-EM index, phased in over a 10-month period, we initiated coverage with an OW in India amid solid fiscal fundamentals, softening growth and inflation well on its way to target. We stay N local duration overall, with OWs in India, Poland, Türkiye and Uruguay offset by a short duration overlay and an UW in Chile and Thailand.

## Credit

Global credit markets remain in ‘The Just Enough World’. Global credit markets’ all-in yields remain just enough to offset thin spreads. Growth might be showing signs of moderating, but remains just enough to allay concerns that we remain in anything but a benign default rate environment; and, even though we’re likely in a shallow rate cut cycle, Fed delivery will be just enough to keep a lid on bond yields and, importantly, rate volatility. In terms of risks through year-end, we highlight: 1) the consumer, 2) risks of weaker foreign demand, 3) US politics (Spread Strategy Spotlight, Jul 12<sup>th</sup>).

Spreads under some pressure from lower yields entering earnings season. US HG bond spreads are only 6bps off the YTD tights as supply has been very light MTD and quarterend rebalancing flows were favorable to HG credit. Spreads will likely be choppier as yields decline alongside increasingly disappointing economic data presaging earlier Fed cuts. One key variable in the coming weeks will be the sensitivity of spreads to rates; the correlation between rates and spreads has been mostly negative over the past year but at times it has flipped and currently has been trending higher. We think on balance that lower yields will be negative for spreads but the magnitude of this may vary (CMOS, Jul 12<sup>th</sup>).

Technicals will be in control in euro credit over the summer, in our view, with ongoing inflows and slowing primary volumes. Euro IG credit 5s10s curves are already extremely flat, which limits the room for long-end spread outperformance even if a disinflation narrative triggers a grab for duration. Subordinated bond spreads would likely underperform initially on lower yields, but quickly catch back up due to strong demand (European Credit Weekly, Jul 12<sup>th</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/d86d601bd2814200aa90d3e67335dfb85a368422f7ec6d5d470420ef0f0511cd.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

Euro HY default rates rebounded from a dip in April to reach 2.8% in June. The issuer-weighted rate rose to 3.8%; both measures are at their highest level since June 2021. The amount of distressed debt has been edging lower, partially due to bonds falling out of the index upon restructuring and not being replaced. Various dispersion measures appear to have peaked in April, and the frequency of outsized price moves has been modest in Q2 (HY Talking Points, Jul 8<sup>th</sup>).

## Currencies

Dollar down but not out: The dollar is overshooting rates and USD length is quite extended (vs. CHF, JPY, CAD, EUR, CNH) which should make us open-minded to a window of USD weakness. However, we continue to think that the pullback in the dollar will not be sustained over the medium term and should be shallow given the uncertain cyclical backdrop outside the US as well, and trade conflict risks from the US elections. Strong USD seasonality during August can offer a tactical pillar of support. A decline in odds of a Trump Presidency or a pick up in European PMIs would challenge our otherwise bullish view on the dollar.

Trade Recommendations: Sell NZD/USD outright. Refresh delta in bearish EUR/USD via put spread. G10 forecasts: all unchanged. EM: USD/ZAR revised down on improving fundamentals post-election to 4Q 17.65 (18.35) (KCV, Jul 12<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

A dichotomy in oil balances. Our balances suggest a \~1.0 mbd deficit in oil liquids in 3Q, comprised of a large 1.6 mbd surplus in products, but a massive 2.4 mbd draw in crude. This dichotomy represents a historic norm in terms of direction, but is an anomaly in terms of scale. Supply and demand so far have performed largely in line with our expectations, but if there is a weak spot in our balances, it would likely be on the refining side (Oil Markets Weekly, Jul 11<sup>th</sup>). A rebound in US and Chinese air travel activity, and a weather-related increase in fuel oil and gasoil use in the Middle East, are adding tailwinds to our July demand projections. OECD commercial oil stocks turn to draw in June (Oil Demand Tracker, Jul 10<sup>th</sup>).

There has been a modest uptick in interest in smaller, often overlooked metals such as zinc, which outperformed the base complex last quarter. Despite very tight zinc supply indicators, for now, anaemic demand lacks any teeth to make tight smelter supply actually bite, and we still project a largely balanced refined zinc market for FY24. We think zinc prices will largely move more sideways through the balance of the year. For 2025, we still see zinc prices participating with the broader base metals rally we anticipate to be driven primarily by copper (Metals Weekly, Jul 11<sup>th</sup>).

Tracking China’s purchases of US ag products and rehashing the US China Trade War. Ahead of the 2024 US Election, US centric agri markets, grains, oilseeds and cotton, are trading at cautiously low levels, amid limited risk appetite and critically tight major exporter grain stocks, albeit comfortable old crop US availability. Investor positioning across US traded agri markets is at the shortest seasonal level in near 15Y, in contrast to grain stocks near critical lows and a rising agri export market share for the US. Our highest-conviction longs are Sugar, Wheat and Corn (Agricultural Markets Weekly, Jul 9<sup>th</sup>).

Haynesville production may provide softness for the US Nat gas balance this winter. The brief pop in Haynesville production doesn't change our view on balance for summer 2024, but we see risk to our production forecast in Nov/Dec 2024. While bearish price psychology prevails, we continue to see upside potential for price. While the US Natural gas appears undervalued to us at the current time relative to our forecast of fundamentals, we caution that view could take some time to play out, especially as uncertainty regarding production continues to linger (US Natural Gas, Jul 12<sup>th</sup>).

## IMF surcharges: Some (limited) fee-ling of relief

• Only 23 countries are currently paying surcharges, which are not very large for most

• While welcoming, changes to surcharges would provide minor relief with some limited exceptions

IMF loans not only come with demands for reforms such as fiscal tightening, but they can also have a high interest rate cost, particularly for large borrowers. According to news agencies, the IMF will review its policies on fees and surcharges after more nations raised concerns. This would not be the first time: back in December 2022, Argentina and Pakistan were pushing for at least a temporary drop of IMF surcharges.

Particularly in the post-COVID period, the IMF was criticised for using surcharges applied to vulnerable countries (note: no low-income country pays surcharges). Calls to remove surcharges escalated as the IMF sharply increased its support globally during the COVID-19 crisis. Increased IMF lending led to surcharges affecting more countries, bringing more profits to the IMF. As global interest rates started to increase in 2022, the overall IMF borrowing cost has surged, particularly for large and persistent borrowers (Figure 1). Recognising the strain that causes, the IMF was considering adjustments to its surcharges ahead of recent headlines.

Below we look at the actual and projected cost of surcharges, in the context of overall borrowing costs from the IMF. Currently, only 23 countries are paying IMF surcharges, mainly frontiers, with Argentina as the largest payer. Reducing debt service through lowering or removing some IMF fees is surely helpful. Yet, as shown below, the macro impact of debt service relief is limited even for top borrowers such as Argentina and Ecuador. There is a more meaningful impact through FX reserves: many of these countries do not have large FX reserves, and even those available have been partly built on borrowing from the IMF, which will have to be repaid.

## Costs of borrowing from the IMF

To borrow from the IMF, a country pays a variable marketbased SDR interest rate (currently 4.01%) plus a 100bp margin. Together they form the basic rate of charge on General Resources Account (GRA) loans (current minimum 5.01%). Besides GRA, the IMF also lends to low-income countries through the Poverty Reduction and Growth Trust (PRGT) and to ongoing programme countries through the Resilience and Sustainability Facility (RSF) if requested. Lending under PRGT carries no interest currently and no surcharges. RSF charges depend on the PRGT status, with a 95bp margin maximum. With higher global rates, the funding from IMF has become a lot more expensive as the SDR standard rate is

linked to CNY, EUR, JPY, GBP, USD rates and exchange rates.

For GRA lending, on top of a 50bp service charge on amount drawn, the IMF uses two types of surcharges as a way to discourage countries from borrowing excessive amounts and/or over long periods. However, the quasi-permanent imbalances present in some economies made them dependent on IMF funding and unable to avoid the surcharges. This is particularly the case for Argentina, Egypt, Ecuador and Ukraine - all large IMF borrowers. As such, the surcharges have lost their preventive role and only became an extra burden.

![](images/2faadf819524c104b665463661e08ee5d91fea3ef663299f2693c2b0fcade9c0.jpg)

In our view, the preventative role of surcharges has been diminished for a long period now. Large paid surcharges reflect the country’s inability to escape IMF support. Yet, removal of surcharges would not generate significant easing of the debt service, except for Argentina and Ecuador (maybe Benin and Pakistan too). It would definitely not carry any meaningful implications for fiscal or debt dynamics anywhere.

On top of the basic rate, a surcharge of 200bp is paid on the amount of credit outstanding above 187.5% of quota. If credit remains above this threshold after 36 months for GRA programmes (51 months for EFF programmes), a further 100bp surcharge is added, taking the total surcharge to 300 basis points. This brings the current overall interest rate to about 8.01%.

SDR allocation could also have an impact on charges. If SDRs are not used, a country pays interest on the SDR allocation but receives an equal interest for SDR holdings. If instead a country decides to use the allocated SDRs (effectively borrowing from the IMF), the imbalance between holdings and allocation involves an interest payment charged at the SDR rate.

The current picture shows as the implied interest cost for the countries affected by surcharges standing between 4.2% (Tunisia and Armenia) and 7.5% (Costa Rica). Our calculation accounts for programme interest payments as well as surcharges, leaving aside SDR allocations and related charges (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Implied interest cost and outstanding credit\*
![](images/9a9c22d0e8d513a59e6dfba67cf9630ae9dd472cdf0a840d5aed55c3761efb07.jpg)
Source: IMF, J.P. Morgan - data as of June 2024 \*countries over 187.5% threshold

## Rather limited potential relief

We asses the relevance of potential relief from surcharge changes as % of GDP and FX reserves. The latter captures situations when buffers are rather low and relief can provide a boost to FX liquidity even if overall macro impact is limited.

The 23 countries currently affected by surcharges are mostly frontier economies with many parts of our EM Edge grouping. Owing to its large and long-standing borrowing from the IMF, Argentina has, by far, the largest projected surcharges. The IMF currently shows projected surcharges payment of US\$5.3bn for Argentina. It is followed by EM Edge countries such as Ecuador, Ukraine and Egypt, with a cumulative US\$1.3bn total. Overall, the saving impact of the potential surcharges removal would have limited macro impact. In relative terms, the surcharges account for a maximum of 0.2% of GDP per year with several countries even below 0.1% of GDP (Table 1). The complete removal of surcharges would be far from a game-changer for fiscal or growth dynamics, or even for debt service, as these countries tend to have large debt stock and/or substantial debt services.

The impact is somewhat more visible when surcharges are compared against FX reserves. The cost is still reduced for most names, but there is a meaningful liquidity impact on Argentina, Ecuador and to a lesser extent Pakistan/Benin. Increased FX buffers would help their financial stability, contributing to better macro outcomes.

A broader, still limited, impact can be achieved through changes to the 100bp margin applied on top of the SDR rate for payments related to previous or ongoing IMF programmes. While benefits can be broader, it is hard to foresee a large move, if any, in the 100bp margin. As a % of GDP, the countries paying the largest interest bill are small such as Suriname and Barbados. However, also some big names such as Ukraine, Argentina and Ecuador have currently projected IMF interest payments over 3% of GDP for the full repayment outlook (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Projected charges payments to IMF\*
![](images/1b2220e50d8e145c37518952b975b52e9c74a4b5afe5f58511e4ca0da26bb54e.jpg)
Source: IMF, J.P. Morgan \*from Jul-24 onwards as of end of Jun-24

## Helping liquidity rather than debt dynamics

Removing (or lowering) IMF surcharges would be a welcome move as their purpose has become stale. Despite its macro impact rather reduced, the move is still beneficial for the affected countries, particularly from a liquidity perspective. For some, there is a meaningful impact on FX reserves (Figure 4), especially in Argentina, Ecuador but also Pakistan and Benin, but most countries will have no choice but to continue to implement reforms. Changes to surcharges cannot have any meaningful impact on debt dynamics.

## Euro area: Fiscal rules imply modest 2025 tightening

• New fiscal rules will shape 2025 national budgets

Exact application of various rules will only be clarified late this year

• Aggregate 2025 fiscal drag likely up to 0.5% of GDP, limiting the growth impact

While for many the focus is still on France, fiscal policy in other Euro area countries will also shape the macro-economic outlook. The required fiscal adjustments depend on the new fiscal rules, which came into force in April 2024 and will affect the budgets from 2025 onwards. Despite some uncertainty about the exact application of the new rules, our original assumption of a 0.5% of GDP structural primary adjustment next year remains a reasonable baseline, and, if anything, the possibility of some further fiscal slippage, including in France, would skew the risk bias to a smaller fiscal adjustment.

Figure 1: Corrective and preventative arms of new EU Fiscal Rules
![](images/b2e9e3cbffa23df6bcf177dc0c76f16cc349b7ca176bcc05c4296463353eb123.jpg)
Source: European Commission, J.P. Morgan

## The new rules

While the EU Treaty continues to be benchmarked on limits of a 3% deficit and a 60% debt level, this leaves open many practical questions, including the speed of adjustments to these limits. As a result, a fiscal framework is needed to operationalise the Treaty limits. The new framework moves away from a prior requirement to reduce the debt down to 60% over 20 years (the “1/20th” rule), which was unrealistic for high debt countries and not complied with. Instead, it is

anchored on medium-term net expenditure paths and specifies expenditure adjustments that put government debt on “plausibly” downward paths. “Plausibly” is informed by a debt sustainability analysis (DSA) that, for example, requires the debt ratio to fall even in the case of negative shocks (i.e., with a sufficiently high probability). This DSA-based requirement for fiscal policy can be superseded if even larger adjustments are needed under the corrective arm or other safeguards under the preventive arm of the new rules (Figure 1).

For countries with deficits exceeding 3% of GDP, a deficitbased excessive deficit procedure (EDP) is typically launched. Under this, the corrective arm of the rules comes into play, and specifies a minimum structural adjustment of 0.5% of GDP per year until the deficit has been reduced to below 3% in a lasting way. To prevent countries having to adjust by more than this to offset the impact of past ECB rate hikes on their interest costs, the required adjustment of 0.5% is on the structural primary balance from 2025 to 2027.

In addition to the requirements from the DSA and the corrective arm, the preventive arm also has additional safeguards that depend on a country’s debt level. For countries with debt exceeding 90% of GDP, debt must be reduced by 1% of GDP on average per year over a four- or seven-year adjustment period (the “debt sustainability safeguard”). For countries with debt between 60% and 90%, the budget must be adjusted so that debt falls by 0.5% of GDP per year on average over a four- or seven-year period. Finally, there is a “deficit resilience safeguard”, which in practice likely applies only to countries with debt above 60%. The aim of this is to provide a buffer relative to the 3% of GDP deficit limit by aiming for a structural deficit of under 1.5% of GDP. Whether a country can adjust over four or seven years depends on it commitments to sufficient reforms and investments. But, irrespective of the adjustment length, debt must be put on a similar downward path and the adjustment of the fiscal position should not be back-loaded over the adjustment period.

Clearly, the various parts of the new rules can give different adjustment requirements. The binding one should be the one necessitating the biggest adjustment, although this will only be clarified during actual implementation. At this point, the required adjustment is translated into a trajectory for a single indicator: net primary expenditure (NPE). This is an updated indicator that is defined as government expenditure minus spending on interest, discretionary revenue measures, some cyclical expenditures, one-offs and expenditures on some EU programmes. This NPE trajectory is set for each country for the next 4-5 years and serves as the basis of the annual budget discussions between the Member State and the European Commission/Council. The stated aim of expressing the adjustment needs in terms of a multi-year path for this indicator is to make the rules simpler, more transparent, under the government’s control, and more clearly anchored on fiscal decisions that have a medium-term horizon. For countries with debt above 60 percent of GDP or a deficit greater than 3 percent of GDP, the European Commission sets a reference trajectory for the NPE at an early stage in the budget process so that the country can integrate it into its budget planning. For others, this is more optional.

Given this is the first round under the new rules, there is some uncertainty about the required adjustment. First, the requirements coming out of the DSA are hard to assess due to its complexity. Second, countries in an EDP could potentially need to do more than the 0.5% of GDP per year adjustment if this does not put debt on a sufficiently rapid downward path, as specified by the requirements of the preventive arm (including the DSA). Ultimately whether countries under an EDP will have to adjust more than the minimum under the corrective arm will only be clarified once the European Commission and Council recommendations are published, following submission of the 2025 Budgets and medium-term fiscal structural plans.

## Who is in which bucket?

As different parts of the overall fiscal governance framework will be binding for different countries, Table 1 shows the

European Commission’s latest Spring fiscal projections. It organises countries by whether they have been proposed for an EDP, and if not, if their debt is over 60% or 90% of GDP. These projections are based on a “no policy change” assumption, i.e. they only incorporate announced measures so far for 2025. Based on this, there are five countries that are projected this year to have a deficit of under 3%, a structural deficit of under 1.5% and debt under 60%. They are the Netherlands, Luxembourg, Ireland, Estonia and Lithuania. They must simply maintain a “prudent” debt trajectory”. This is sensitive to the exact assumption made, for example on interest rates, but in general these countries do not face obvious adjustment needs based on their medium-term fiscal outlooks.

Germany is in a similar position. Its debt is currently still slightly above 60%, but its deficit is well below 3% and its structural budget position meets the deficit resilience safeguard. Arguably, Germany may need to adjust a bit further if an interest rate above the J.P. Morgan forecast is assumed, but, Germany will be making this adjustment anyway over the next 1-2 years of perhaps about ¾% of GDP in total in order to comply with its domestic debt brake, which is more constraining than the EU fiscal rules. It specifies a maximum structural budget deficit of 0.35 percent of GDP.

A few other countries are also in favourable positions. This group includes Greece, Portugal and Cyprus. They are all seeing rapid declines in their debt ratios, albeit from higher levels, and in the case of Portugal and Cyprus may even have some scope to ease policy modestly over time.

Significant adjustments are needed in the five countries (France, Italy, Belgium, Slovakia and Malta) for which the European Commission has proposed opening Excessive Deficit Procedures (EDP). In addition, significant adjustment needs exist in Austria, Finland, Slovenia and Croatia. Together, they make up 55% of Euro area GDP.

Quantifying the exact adjustment requirements for these countries is not easy. Under the corrective arm of the new rules, the five EDP countries would need to adjust their structural (primary) positions by a minimum of 0.5% per year until their deficits are under 3% of GDP. This does not suffice, however, to put their debt on downward trajectories, with Belgium possibly being closest. But for Belgium, the extent of any debt reduction comes late and is too modest to meet the 1%-pt per year debt to GDP decline under the preventive arm. And, more generally, there is still some uncertainty about how the DSA will be applied.

To try to arrive at an initial and rough quantification, we take our own interest rate assumptions, which have tended to be lower than that of the European Commission; and assume that output gaps close relatively quickly, feedback loops from any fiscal adjustment via growth are negligible, and that the Euro pean Commission’s latest Spring Forecast correctly sets the starting positions in terms of the 2024 structural positions. Based on this, the total adjustments could be up to 4% of GDP in France, up to 5% of GDP in Italy, up to 3% of GDP in Belgium, up to 4% of GDP in Slovakia and up to 1.5%-pt of GDP in Malta. Malta is special in the sense that it has only a deficit problem, but not a debt problem. Beyond the EDP countries, the total adjustment needs are likely in the ballpark of 1.5% of GDP in Spain, 1.5% in Austria, 2% in Finland, 0.75% in Slovenia, 1% in Latvia and 1% in Croatia. Bruegel has also produced some estimates and they are roughly in line with those presented here. All of this assumes seven-year adjustment periods and the appropriate caveats apply.

## What does it mean for growth?

Given this will be the first year under the new rules following a prolonged period under the general escape clause, there is still uncertainty about how the total adjustments needs will be quantified and how quickly they have to be implemented. Ultimately, however, the debt path needs to be put on a sufficiently downward trajectory, irrespective of the adjustment period agreed. In some countries, the NGEU will help to maintain a higher level of public investment spending, but it is less clear whether this changes the fiscal thrust, which depends more on the change in overall spending from one year to the next. It is possible that the deployment of NGEU grants is still gathering pace in some countries, but we suspect that the impact on the Euro area’s fiscal thrust will be modest over the next 1-2 years, in line with Commission estimates in its Spring 2024 forecast.

Aggregating across euro area countries, a first stab at applying the new fiscal rules suggests that the Euro area will need to see an overall tightening of fiscal policy of around 0.5% of GDP in 2025, and that this will need to be continued for another 3-4 years. For 2025, the current Commission estimates and those of the European Fiscal Board (on unchanged polices) suggest a closer to neutral fiscal impulse in 2025, but these estimates are based on a “no policy change” assumption, rather than on the draft 2025 Budgets that are due to be published in the autumn.

Indeed, a lot will depend on the discussions this autumn, which will reveal how the European Commission will apply parts of the fiscal framework. There could also be delays given this is the first time countries are being asked to produce medium-term fiscal structural plans. If the Commission wants to holistically assess EDPs, 2025 Budgets and medium-term plans together, then the process could potentially get delayed till early next year, and given political constraints to reducing current spending beyond the roll-off of energy support measures, further fiscal slippages may be possible, for example in France.

Overall, there will be a process of learning by doing and potentially tense discussions between member states and the Commission later this year. In a broader sense, we suspect the pressure to apply the new rules rigorously will be significant as the European Commission will be extremely conscious of the need to establish its credibility and as financial markets will be closely watching. But, given the likely size of any contractionary fiscal impulse, we don’t expect it to have a material impact on growth in 2025, which should be underpinned by a further rotation to private demand.

## France: Politics in a hung parliament

• Lower house opens next week; parties trying to build larger groups to lead the government

• Any government, however, looks weak as likely coalitions look too small

• Macron calling instead for cross-party grand coalition

• Budget rolls on in worst-case scenario

The result of the French lower house election was a big surprise. Polls since the start of the election process had suggested that the National Rally (RN, far-right) would come first, although only with a relative majority. While RN did worse than expected, both NFP (centre-left to far-left alliance) and ENS (Macron’s alliance, centre) did better than expected in the second round of voting. This can be explained by a high turnout of voters and the decision of many candidates to drop out of the second round of voting in favor of the candidate best positioned to win against RN.

Table 1: French lower house composition
<table><tr><td>Number of seats</td></tr><tr><td>NFP (centre-left to far-left) 182</td></tr><tr><td>ENS (Macron&#x27;s coalition, centre) 168</td></tr><tr><td>RN (National Rally, far right) 143</td></tr><tr><td>LR (Republicans, right) 46</td></tr><tr><td>Other right 14</td></tr><tr><td>Other left 13</td></tr><tr><td>Other centre 6</td></tr><tr><td>Others 5</td></tr><tr><td>Total 577</td></tr><tr><td>Participation rate (%) 66.6</td></tr></table>

Source: French government, J.P.Morgan

The outcome of the French lower house election is a hung parliament (Table 1). The house is very split across parties, with 182 seats for NFP, 168 seats for ENS, 143 seats for RN, 45 seats for LR (Republicans, right) and 39 MPs not included in these alliances (split across left and right). A hung parliament has no precedent in the current institutional regime and it is unclear how this political configuration will operate.

We provide some markers below for the coming weeks. Coalitions are trying to attract MPs and Macron will be pressured to nominate a PM from the biggest group when the house opens on July 18. These groups, however, will be too small to back a stable government, thus Macron is pushing for a cross-party grand coalition before nominating a new PM. The current government will act as caretaker until a new government is formed.

## From stability to instability

The institutional set up in France normally makes it easy to get an absolute majority in the lower house, with some exceptions. In the last two years, for example, Macron only had a relative majority (250 MPs compared to an absolute majority of 289). A hung parliament, as France faces now, has never happened since the start of the fifth republic in 1958.

According to the constitution, the president nominates a prime minister who is backed by a majority of MPs in the lower house (even if this majority is from the opposition, which leads to period of cohabitation; more on this below). As a result, bills presented by the government (prime minister and cabinet members) can be voted through the lower house and become law.

A hung parliament, as currently seen, makes the legislative process hard. There is no majority to pass laws. The government can also easily fall following a motion of no confidence. And such a motion can easily be triggered. A tenth of MPs in the house (i.e. 58 at least) can call for a motion of no confidence. Using article 49.3 (i.e. the government bypassing the house to pass a law, details here) also triggers a motion of no confidence. For the government to fall, a majority of MPs (i.e. at least 289) must vote in favour of the motion of no confidence.

With less than 200 MPs, any coalition in the house as currently seen could easily fall, as it would face nearly 400 MPs in a motion of no confidence. With 250 MPs, the Macron government nearly fell when it faced such a motion.

## Lines could move in the short term

The left alliance NFP came first in the election and claims that it should lead the next government. Internal talks within the coalition are taking place to name a new PM, but no easy agreement is in sight. This coalition includes four parties, with LFI (Melenchon’s party) having most seats but not by a wide margin. The socialist party, centre-left, is second.

The house will open on July 18 and each MP will have to join a group on that day. The composition of groups may be different to the composition we have now based on election results. Some MPs have already stated that they would leave their party to join a different group. Parties are also generally trying to attract MPs from other parties to build a larger group, or are trying to form coalitions. Any MP can change group at any time.

On July 18, one possibility is that the left alliance NFP remains the biggest group in the house. Another possibility is that Macron’s party attracts enough MPs (probably from the

Republicans party) to create a larger group than the left alliance. The biggest group will claim that it should lead the government.

## No simple route to stability

In the current context, any government based on existing coalitions looks likely to be weak, in our view. The legislative process would be impaired, and the government could easily fall.

Macron is trying to avoid such an outcome. In a letter published in the press this week, Macron argued that the house was very split, and that no coalition had won the election since no coalition as they stand would able to generate a stable government. As a result, Macron asked MPs to come up with a cross-party majority (grand coalition) that would back a stable government and ensure a more normal working of the institutions.

Macron also mentioned in this letter that it could take time to get a new PM and that the current government would act as a caretaker in the meantime. This suggests, in our view, that Macron could wait to nominate a new PM, although the political pressure will be strong to do so when the house opens on July 18.

In our view, the only stable solution, given the composition of the house, is a grand coalition (spanning centre-left to the centre-right). But party incentives may not lead to such an outcome. The alternative is a technocratic government until a grand coalition emerges or until the next lower house election.

The power to call a new lower house election is in the hands of the president, but with an important caveat: the president cannot call a new lower house election for a year. It is not clear, however, that a new election would provide a more solid majority for a new government.

## The specifics of cohabitation

While a grand coalition seems the only stable outcome for a new government, it is possible that such a solution does not emerge in the short term. If NFP is able to form a government after the house opens, this would lead to a period of cohabitation, when the government and president are from opposing parties. The balance of power in this case is specific.

In normal times, the majority in the house is aligned with the president’s party. The president is then free to choose the prime minister and nominates cabinet members on the advice of the prime minister. The president in this context has full powers: she/he leads the government, which is managed by

the prime minister.

On occasions, the majority in the lower house is not aligned with the president’s party, but instead comes from the opposition. This leads to a period of “cohabitation”, which has occurred three times during the Fifth Republic (1986-88 and 1993-95 when Mitterrand was president, and 1997-2002 when Chirac was president). In this scenario, the president needs to work with a prime minister from the opposition. The prime minister then selects cabinet members.

During a cohabitation, powers are split between the president and the government (i.e. prime minister and cabinet members). The government runs the domestic agenda and the president’s role is limited to external affairs, defense and justice.

For domestic affairs, the interaction between the president and the government may not be smooth. In practice, the government action can be slowed by the president. For example, the NFP coalition claims that it wants to legislate this summer. But the house is in recess and the government would need the president to agree to open an extraordinary session of the house. Macron does not have to and the legislative process would only start in October (the ordinary session). Also, after a law has been agreed by the parliament (normally the lower and upper house would have a look at it twice), the president can ask this text to be reviewed again by the two houses. Finally, the president can ask the constitutional court to provide some advice on the legal basis of the law. But, while the president can slow the legislative process, he does not have veto power.

## A reminder on the budget

While the period ahead may be politically uncertain, France will move forward with a budget. There are various possibilities for a budget to go through. It could be voted in parliament (which looks unlikely in the current circumstances). The government could bypass the house and pass the budget bill into law by invoking article 49.3 of the constitution, but doing so risks the government falling as the house in return can trigger a motion of no confidence. The government could push the bill by invoking article 47 of the constitution, which gives a shorter amount of time for the upper and lower house to assess the budget (70 days) and implies that the bill would become effective as an ordinance if the house does not provide any decision within that time. Or the existing budget rolls on (based on article 47) as the government would for an urgent matter ask the parliament for authorization to collect taxes and provide funds to the administration if no budget has been agreed before the start of the fiscal year (i.e. January 1<sup>st</sup> ). We assume that a majority of MPs would be pragmatic and agree to that request.

## Mexico: The GDP-domestic demand divide

• After a good run in 2022 and most of 2023, GDP has slowed

Domestic demand is strong but external demand is an ever growing drag

• Wage, fiscal, and monetary policies are largely responsible for this divide

• Though absent now, all else equal, imbalances seem bound to build

For a while we have been concerned by Mexico’s inability to regain the rapid growth pace seen in 2022/23. In late last year growth hit a pothole, and sluggishness lingered into 1Q24. This is difficult to reconcile with robust supports for demand. On the consumer side these come from very large gains in financial wealth plus a sustained rapid increase in income flow. For businesses, there are low levels of debt and improved profitability - hence Banxico’s index of firms’ vulnerability is at a historic low. Some data have certainly been consistent with this view: domestic demand is still booming, growing roughly 4.5% annualized in 1Q, well above the just above-1% gain in GDP in the same period. There are just two possible explanations for this disconnect: statistical mis-measurement or an extremely heavy external drag. It has been the latter (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Demand side GDP breakdown
![](images/9da055c032eaba0b4ecb42d706c50f8aa2b9bad2b0be9eb65bb7b180ebc33cbe.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan with data from INEG

A key development is the shift in the composition of the economy brought by the ongoing wage policy (see here), which is also being boosted by fiscal spending reallocation toward social spending (subsidies, pensions, transfers). This has led to a massive shift in national income distribution in favor of households and has been a boon for consumption. However, amid dysfunctional credit and interest rate transmission channels, Banxico’s only way (besides through managing inflation expectations) to cool the economy and bring down inflation has been through an appreciating currency . By promoting an overvalued currency it has been able to suppress tradable prices and restrain net external demand. But this seems an unsustainable equilibrium, as we analyze below.

## Strong consumer, strong businesses

The balance sheets of both businesses and consumers are in a very good shape. Consumers have seen a substantial increase vs. pre-pandemic in their financial wealth, best defined in Banxico’s “households’ financial position”(Figure 2), defined as financial assets less financial liabilities—it leaves out real assets such as real estate, for example .

Figure 2: Household financial position % of GDP
![](images/b98c0f12901ae944e48c2f4d59f33425ffa429d9c9b538c9aac5e2488baa1648.jpg)

Households are not only more wealthy - at the same time their real income flow has shifted significantly higher thanks to a real wage income increase. The best indicator of this is the rapid rise in the share of labor in national income relative to other production factors/sectors (or taxes).Wage policies pursued by the government have driven this large shift, and will not be abandoned anytime soon (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Labor share in national income % of national income, 4q rolling average

![](images/79bbd12429b7423b719113bedfa089bb1a7caa48ad2ef41c5613649706cf0155.jpg)

Businesses have been favored also by a host of forces, ranging from improved profitability (at list among listed companies) and high savings to low debt and a cyclical thrust. Note that although profits’ share of national income has declined, overall rapid growth of aggregate national income still makes for positive profits growth. Current firms’ “vulnerabilities” are seen as being below average from a historical standpoint and their probability of defaulting on loans as low as 1%, 1.5%-pts below the pre-pandemic level (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Vulnerability index of listed companies Index
![](images/a14bc76cce8ed560fea928d900ae5cf6cd3a314a96c98403c2d6cf204cc67d9d.jpg)

Public-related works by the outgoing administration have also markedly helped. States where these projects have taken place accounted for about 8%-pts out of 17%oya in construction output in 2023 (despite representing only about 8% of GDP). However, the rapid build-up of real estate construction evident in the large contribution to construction output in regions completely unrelated to public projects and home to important manufacturing hubs also point to nearshoring activity. As we’ve noted before—these states accounted for just over 7%-pts of the increase in construction output last year. However, this nearshoring thrust could soon find its limits due to shortages of key inputs such as water and electricity, or generally speaking deficient infrastructure.

## The curse in disguise

As with most central banks globally, Banxico faced a surge in inflation after the immediate disinflation brought by the pandemic. However, in addition, the above mentioned dynamics led to very robust domestic demand and added an additional layer of complexity. Wage and fiscal policies were additional sources of inflation pressure, as evident in still-high services inflation currently. Banxico’s response was swift, significantly tightening its policy stance, reaching (and still being close to) real rates of 7.5%.

The credit and interest rate monetary policy transmission channels in Mexico are known for not being significantly functional, as credit penetration is low, mortgage credit is issued in long-term fixed rate almost entirely and consumer credit lending rates are almost completely unresponsive to changes in the policy rate. The real adjustment to inflation comes through the expectations and exchange rate channels. With real rates being so high, carry gains from the currency helped explained its massive appreciation. Additional supports for the currency have come from relatively sound fiscal accounts (Mexico did not engage in stimulus during the pandemic), prospects of nearshoring, remittances and, more recently, tourism-related inflows and reduced oil imports. But as we’ve pointed out before, the real exchange rate (in our view) went too far when compared with some of its fundamentals (Figure 5).

Figure 5: MX/US relative productivity and REER
![](images/22d60268f5141bc1de8a1db806cd5ef5648ff75e0cbf69595c8b4ee88fb73cac.jpg)

Exchange rate appreciation has generated slower core goods inflation, particularly nonfood goods inflation, given that this component is the most reactive to FX given its large content of imported items (or items that compete with imports). The flip side to rapid core goods disinflation was a massive substitution of domestically produced goods (national goods) for imported goods, evident in both consumer and capital goods spending (Figures 6 and 7).

Figure 6: Real imported and national goods consumption Index, sa (2018=100)
![](images/665f4d7baaa635781884e49cfef15ca10b53af4b059243d8d0a90522ec37722e.jpg)

Figure 7: Gross fixed investment - M&Eq Index, sa (2017 =100)
![](images/6b801bece1a0794c82e3db27754f794a9e64e7d2861bb19cc903618cc639e4e6.jpg)

To put this in perspective, since the overvaluation of the REER became more noticeable vs. our measure of fundamentals back in January 2022, consumption of goods produced domestically has not grown at all. By contrast, real consumption of imported goods has risen about 56% over the same time span. Equally, while real spending on domestic produced capital goods is below its 2017 level, real imported capex is

up nearly 36%. Overall, in the last four quarters, net exports have subtracted 5%-pts from over-year-ago GDP growth (Figure 8).

Figure 8: Net external demand %-pt contribution to GDP %oya growth
![](images/37cd428ecd861621f3cf8c421b8046ae87522ed308cab9afd999beb4925d36cf.jpg)

## There’s no silver bullet

There are several issues with the current growth model being pursued. In the past we’ve dealt with the imbalances that could arise from the composition of domestic demand itself. Wage policies should continue to fuel consumption, but this has thus far just started to be noticeably accompanied by investment spending, and, hence, labor productivity is still lagging real wage growth. The risk of impeding private sector investment remains alive, and that would set the limits on just how much higher wages can go without creating imbalances, particularly in the form of inflation and/or a collapse in employment levels.

There is separate brewing imbalance that derives from that attempt to quell inflation pressures against the backdrop of robust domestic demand amid an incomplete monetary policy transmission channel. In our view, it is positive that Banxico’s sharp tightening of policy cooled inflation through a massive appreciation of the REER. But prior to the recent sell-off, and currently still, this process seems to have gone too far. In our view, there are already signs of an underlying deterioration in the current account, which has thus far been masked by large remittances inflows. However, the latter are no longer growing in nominal terms. The potential shift from oil importer to self-sufficiency could bring some relief (part of it has already happened), but it is not straightforward (we will delve into this matter later on). On a real basis, national spending is being met increasingly by imports due to the overly appreciated level of the real exchange rate.

In the most extreme case, a sustained degree of FX-induced substitution toward imports like the one we are currently witnessing will raise increasingly strong challenges for domestic companies competing in these market segments, raising the possibility of them actually going out of business. From a medium-term perspective, we think clearer industrial policies that foster investment and help maximize relocation opportunities and raise labor productivity would go a long way toward quelling these potential imbalances.

In the short term, we think that the recent loss of momentum in services inflation (in turn a function of domestic demand growth being off its very high peak) offers the possibility for Banxico to remove policy restriction to allow the FX to weaken to levels more aligned with fundamentals. This would certainly raise tradable prices, but should be compensated by non-tradable prices while allowing for a healthy rebalancing in the sources of growth between domestic and external; in other words, for domestic demand to be met by domestic supply, helping bring closer GDP and domestic demand growth. Alternatively, and optimally, the FX commission could decide to introduce a program to accumulate reserves with the intent to weaken the currency and still maintain restriction through other channels (even if transmission is weak), but this seems politically unpalatable (recall the final word in the FX commission lies with the Ministry of Finance).

## SSA: Inflation persistence will delay monetary easing

1H24 saw significant upside revisions to our inflation forecasts in SSA with the exception of Kenya

Persistent currency pressures were at the heart of the higher inflation partly on BoP pressures

• Nigeria, Angola and Zambia policymakers likely to maintain tightening pace into 2H24...

• … while rate cuts are likely to be pushed backed until late 3Q/4Q in Ghana and Kenya

In our 2024 year ahead (YA) outlook, we had projected a firm disinflation path especially during 2H24 for most of the key SSA economies. This inflation profile was partly predicated on strong base effects given the inflation acceleration witnessed for most, in particular during 2H23, but also reflected fairly benign currency forecasts and slower inflation momentum. Although we had flagged that the main threat to disinflation in the region is likely to come from currency pressures, FX adjustments are proving sizeable this year particularly in Nigeria and Ghana. Only Kenya has seen a significant appreciation in its currency this year, and as such, our inflation profile is softer compared to the rest where we have lifted inflation profiles meaningfully (Table 1).

Table 1: Inflation, FX and policy rate
<table><tr><td colspan="3">Inflation (average)</td><td colspan="2">FX (vs USD)</td><td colspan="2">Policy rate (end period)</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>2024f YA</td><td>Current 2024f</td><td>FX end- Current 23 FX</td><td>% chg YTD</td><td>2024f YA</td><td>Current 2024f</td></tr><tr><td>Nigeria</td><td>22.30</td><td>31.40</td><td>892.48 1553.72</td><td>42.56</td><td>19.75</td><td>27.25</td></tr><tr><td>Zambia</td><td>11.40</td><td>15.30</td><td>25.92 25.34</td><td>-2.28</td><td>10.75</td><td>14.50</td></tr><tr><td>Ghana</td><td>19.30</td><td>22.50</td><td>11.97 15.40</td><td>22.25</td><td>22.00</td><td>27.00</td></tr><tr><td>Angola</td><td>16.60</td><td>27.60</td><td>837.01 881.58</td><td>5.05</td><td>18.00</td><td>20.00</td></tr><tr><td>Kenya</td><td>6.80</td><td>5.50</td><td>158.88 128.96</td><td>-23.20</td><td>9.50</td><td>12.00</td></tr></table>

Source: Various Central Banks, Bloomberg LLP, J.P.Morgan, FX:- means appreciation

Using various models for food and core inflation, we find that FX pass-through is meaningful for both food and core prices. Food prices have also been affected by domestic (climatic) factors. In turn, as food accounts for the biggest chunk in the inflation basket, inflationary pressures have been sustained. For countries already on a disinflation path, i.e. Ghana, we now expect the path to be sticky and non-linear. In economies such as Nigeria, Zambia and Angola, calling the inflation peak has become more challenging - whilst we still expect a peak within the next 1-2 months, the disinflation path that follows will also be quite sticky with risks of more upside than downside.

These inflation revisions have also had an impact on our policy rate calls. Nigeria, Zambia and Angola have kept hiking rates this year to deal with the ensuing pressures and there is likely more to come over the next couple of months as currency pressures are yet to meaningfully abate. In Ghana and Kenya, where we had thought there could be some room to cut rates during 1H24, rate cut now appear only likely during 4Q, with the Kenya Central Bank recently introducing US FED interest rate decisions as a key risk factor for monetary policy formulation and direction. This is despite ex-ante real rates at +750bp, which is the highest level in at least a decade.

## Inflation is still elevated

Cost push pressures, loose fiscal policies and FX weakness are key factors behind the inflation persistence witnessed in the region. Nigeria and Angola in 2023 implemented some fiscal reforms by way of (partial) fuel subsidy removal which led to a significant jump in fuel prices with ripple effects also lingering for longer. The social backlashes that followed also meant that authorities needed to slow down the pace of reforms while also stepping up social interventions which is putting further pressures on fiscal balances. Zambia, on the other hand, is dealing with its worst drought in decades and the impact has been/and is expected to remain significant on inflation and the current account owing to higher food and electricity imports.

That said, recent inflationary pressures in the region have been driven by FX weakness. Balance of payment (BoP) pressures have intensified for some, against the backdrop of tight global financial conditions. As such, FX adjustments have been meaningful, partly also to correct overvalued exchange rates. In a recent EM Edge data watch piece, we flagged that six edge economies have particularly weak external positions (ie. negative extended basic balances). Three of those six are in SSA namely, Nigeria, Zambia and Kenya. However, the latter was able to tap the Eurobond market earlier this year and with renewed interest in local bonds and large multi-lateral inflows, pressures have been quelled.

Figure 1: Nominal FX performance vs. USD
![](images/da04859a68b454c4d16b5128448e6584dd5fde39e031f840102737c514bf805c.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, J.P. Morgan, NGAp- parallel market rates

In Angola, despite running a CA surplus, FX has continued to weaken owing to increased local dollar demand and as repayment needs are large, central bank interventions have been

limited to preserve FX reserves. Low FX liquidity in the official market is also creating some distortions with a parallel market FX rate premium now developing. All told, FX passthrough to inflation remain significant for the region. Nigeria, Ghana and Angola have the highest inflation rates in the region and these economies have also seen the largest nominal FX depreciation in recent years (Figure 2).

FX change % Dec-21 vs May-24
Figure 2: Headline inflation
![](images/d2de9b011a5c814bf2ea4319837d5049367840a9b1289686fa03845a328cba3f.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, J.P. Morgan

## Estimating the FX impact

We estimated some equations for food and core inflation as the dependent variables. Given there isn’t a consistent core inflation variable for the countries, we estimated core inflation to exclude food, energy, transportation and alcohol. After running OLS regressions, we find that FX impact on core prices was strongest in Ghana while it appeared FX passthrough was not as strong in Nigeria. For robustness, we went further to use the VAR model, where results showed the cumulative impact of FX on core inflation is strongest in Ghana and Zambia as a 10% FX shock will deliver about a 2.9% and 2.1% impact on core prices respectively after 12 months (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Core CPI VAR model results for 10% FX shock
![](images/c97511f7ae3b98158e714d8b39721e4b6304caf52791468072181c4dda2d0ea6.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, Nigeria: used parallel market FX rate

Nigeria and Angola responses are similar at around 1% impact after 12 months - for the former, we used available data for parallel market FX rates given periodic official-parallel market premiums. We used a limited data set for Kenya, given some data distortions pre-2018. Invariably, we acknowledge these models face some limitations such as limited data scope, lagged adjustments to regulated prices and, largely, the lack of a flexible exchange rate market, which tends to create periodic FX market distortions.

We used the same approach for food inflation but added global food price (FAO Index) as an additional dependent variable. Global food prices didn’t show any strong correlation to food CPI except with long lags for all the countries. This is partly explained by inherent subsidies in some of the countries. Ghana and Zambia again showed the strongest and quickest impact with a 10% change in FX translating to a 0.9% impact in Ghana and 0.4% in Zambia. This is evident from the significant rise in food inflation recorded in Ghana post the FX depreciation episode from mid 2021 and also the FX depreciation episodes of 2019-2020 in Zambia and even more recently (Figure 4). Domestic factors also play some role in food inflation direction in these countries. Climatic factors in recent years - droughts in Kenya, Angola and Zambia, flooding in Nigeria, have also biased food inflation higher in the region.

Figure 4: FX change vs food inflation
![](images/83606ccf8026dcd854f17d67d0176bf1355db79a011d3528775347f877b1f037.jpg)

## Monetary policy response

Central banks in the region have reacted strongly to FX and inflationary pressures in recent years but policy transmission often tends to be weak. As FX pass-through is a significant inflation driver, central banks’ reaction function tend to focus heavily on currency moves. That said, Kenya could be touted as a positive story for monetary policy implementation in the last two years as an accelerated pace of tightening led inflation lower and eased currency pressures.

However, the inflation outlook for the other countries remain challenging amid ensuing pressures. Despite the disinflation we expect, inflation is unlikely to return to central bank targets even in 12 months time, necessitating central banks to keep the tightening stance for longer. Nigeria, Angola and Zambia have tightened this year. We forecast further tightening still to come for Nigeria (+100bp), Zambia (+50bp) and Angola (+50bp), while we push back rate cuts in Ghana and Kenya to September and December, respectively.

## United States

• June CPI fell 0.1%, core up only 0.1%, capping off a more favorable 2Q

• We now look for a first Fed cut in September, followed by quarterly cuts thereafter

• Activity data continue to hold in as jobless claims retraced the recent move up

• Next week brings another check-up on the consumer with June retail sales

Through much of ’22 and ’23 Fed chair Powell opined that there was a narrow path to a soft landing. In that period we usually (but not always) shared his cautious optimism. In recent weeks that path to a soft landing seems to have gotten a little wider. The latest evidence was the remarkably benign 0.1% increase in the June core CPI, capping a three-month period when the core CPI increased at only a 2.1% annual rate. This stands in pleasant contrast to the three months ending in March, when the same metric ran at a 4.5% rate.

The sharp difference between these two 3-month run rates could justifiably raise concerns that the latest improvement merely reflects volatility in the data. But one encouraging aspect of the latest slowing in inflation is that it has occurred in the stickier components of inflation; most notably the longawaited slowing in rental inflation now appears to be underway. The latest wage data and inflation expectations numbers have also come in lower, bolstering the fundamental case for an easing in inflation pressures.

With the risks of higher inflation becoming embedded appearing to recede, there has been more attention to whether the Fed has overcooked the economy, with the lagged effects of higher rates slowing the economy more than is consistent with a soft landing. The solid 206,000 increase in June employment reported last week suggests business activity growth remains durable. While this week didn’t bring much in the way of activity data, the latest easing in jobless claims (albeit for the noisy July 4<sup>th</sup> week) eased some concerns raised by the recent drift higher in that series.

After the June CPI report we pulled forward our expectation for a first Fed rate cut by one meeting to the September FOMC meeting, still followed by a quarterly pace of rate cuts thereafter. Is there a case for cutting at the late July meeting? Perhaps, but we’re not spending much time thinking about that risk as Fed rhetoric has not teed up that outcome, and absent a significant deterioration in financial conditions we don’t see the Powell Fed as inclined to deliver a policy surprise.

Instead, we think the risk worth spending more time thinking about is that cuts happen at every meeting, rather than every other meeting. In his semiannual monetary policy testimony before Congress this week, Chair Powell continued to sound somewhat dovish, noting that the labor market is in balance and that inflation isn’t the only risk they face. In principle, if the Fed’s employment and inflation risks are in balance policy should be at or near neutral, to more nimbly respond when risks slip out of balance.

In the June dot plot, the highest estimate of the neutral rate, proxied by the longer-run dot, was 3.75%. This is 175bp below the top of the current target range for the funds rate. At a quarterly pace of rate cuts it would take at least a year and a half to get back to a neutral policy stance. That strikes us as a fairly leisurely pace and we can see the case for a faster return to neutral. All that said, the outcome of the upcoming election could bring risks to either side of the Fed’s mandate and so policy gradualism may make sense in an environment of heightened uncertainty.

While activity data were scarce this week, that will change next week, notably with Tuesday’s retail sales report for June. The consumer had been a source of strength in ’23 but spending growth has slowed in ’24. We think lower vehicle sales and nominal gas station spending will pull the headline lower, but away from these two categories we look for a trend-like 0.3% increase in spending. Powell speaks on Monday, the last major Fed communication before the blackout period ahead of the late July FOMC meeting.

## A very good CPI report

The June CPI report was very benign. The headline figure fell 0.06% (expected up 0.1%) and the core figure was up only 0.06% (expected up 0.2%). The headline figure was pulled down by energy prices, which fell 2.0%, thanks in part to a 3.8% drop in gasoline prices. Food prices were up 0.2%, a little firmer than the recent trend.

Within the core component, the long-awaited slowing in both tenants’ and owners’ equivalent rental inflation took place last month: Tenants’ rent increased only 0.26% and owners’ equivalent rent was up 0.28%—both outturns were the lowest since 2021 (Figure 1). Signals in industry data about new rents suggest that much of this cooling in rental inflation should persist.

Figure 1: CPI rent measures
![](images/57429a1942a92a26cd17e05a05b28a6a6ddf78ff2e7f11a606dd3b0508e86f0e.jpg)

Core services ex-rents slipped 0.05%, the second consecutive decline (Figure 2). Within this so-called supercore component, airfares slid 5.0% last month, though vehicle insurance prices increased 0.9% after a welcome decline in May. Medical care service inflation slowed to 0.2% last month.

Figure 2: Core services CPI ex. tenants' rent and OER
![](images/c04aedca20b5784fdc2f6308b222c89b876e44c4b17763b6ebd3b842ba60775e.jpg)

Incorporating the information from the June CPI and PPI reports we estimate that the June core PCE figure (the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge) was up 0.17% last month, leaving the year-ago increase steady at 2.6%.

Looking ahead, standard inflation models project medium-run inflation predicated on three driving variables: labor market slack, inflation expectations, and supply shocks that are usually globally determined. In last week’s June employment report we saw a little more labor market slack open up, with the unemployment rate moving up another tick to 4.1%, and average hourly earnings growth moderating to 3.9% on a year-ago basis.

This week’s Indeed wage tracker for June was unchanged at 3.1% over a year ago (Figure 3). This measure, initially developed by the Central Bank of Ireland and cited by Chair Powell, tracks posted wages and salaries and has been shown to be a good leading indicator of measures like the Atlanta Fed’s wage tracker. If realized wage growth cooled to 3.1% that would be pretty close to Goldilocks: under 3% might raise concerns about the durability of consumer spending, too much above 3.5% would reignite inflation concerns.

Figure 3: Wage trackers
![](images/4f5019719d93f1404a60d49e627ea446aa46f7374e70c3adf3caaeb35f7e6b0d.jpg)

Survey measures of inflation expectations have generally also been well-behaved recently. The NY Fed’s survey measure of year-ahead inflation expectations slipped two ticks to 3.0% while the three-year ahead measure moved up one tick to 2.9%: both one- and three-year ahead measures are now right at the medians for those series, which began in 2013 (Figure 4). The preliminary University of Michigan measures for oneand five-year ahead inflation expectations both moved down a tick to 2.9%.

OFigure 4: NY Fed median consumer inflation expectations
![](images/46ad1cb11d6ff37eb7ef81107734fd977b4cfb019282b2e2706b4606cb9ab063.jpg)

Next week we learn more about the third leg of the inflation factors listed earlier—global supply shocks—with the June import price index report. Like many inflation measures, nonfuel import prices surprised to the upside early in the year before retreating 0.3% in May. We look for a modest 0.1% gain in next week’s report. Broad dollar strength over the last three months should act as a restraint on import prices in coming months.

In addition to next week’s retail sales report, we’ll also want to keep an eye on the jobless claims report. Claims generally drifted higher in May and June before tumbling by 17,000 in the first week of July to 222,000. Continuing claims also modestly retreated in the latest report but remain near twoyear highs.

1. Total ex. gasoline, automotive dealers, building materials, and food serv

We forecast nominal retail sales contracted 0.5% in June, largely due to declines in sales at gasoline stations and motor vehicle and parts dealers. Gasoline prices declined about 4% on the month, and we look for a decline in sales at gasoline stations of a nearly similar magnitude. Unit auto sales slid nearly 4% on the month as well, and we look for a 2.2% drop in nominal sales at motor vehicle and parts dealers. Sales in the closely-watched control group (which excludes autos, gasoline, building materials, and food services) should post a solid 0.3% rise in June, based in part on the signal from our Chase card data. Our forecast foresees some further cooling in household spending during the second half of this year as the labor market cools further, but the near-term indicators still point to solid spending on goods outside of autos and gasoline stations.
