We anticipate the import price index was effectively unchanged in June, as imported fuels prices likely fell for the second straight month. Away from fuels, we expect the import prices index rose 0.1% in June, supported by a modest rebound in food prices after a notable decline in May and firmness in non-fuel industrial supplies. Outside of food and fuels, we expect import prices for the categories of autos, consumer goods excluding autos, and capital goods to be flat to slightly down on the month. Despite the generally soft readings for the monthly changes in the headline and non-fuel import price indexes, both are expected to accelerate in yearago terms in June, with the headline inflation rate for import prices rising 0.2%-pt to 1.3%, and the non-fuel inflation rate climbing 0.4%-pt to 0.9%. If realized, both would mark their highest levels in a year and a half.

We believe that housing starts declined 2.1% to 1.25mn in June, while permits inched up 0.1% to 1.40mn. Both starts and permits have trended lower in recent months, with starts declining in May to their lowest annualized level since the pandemic recovery began, while May permits reached their lowest annualized level in about one and a half years. The slowdown recently has been concentrated in single-family units, which are both a much larger share of the total than multi-family, and which have peaked early this year. We look for single-family starts to have slid 2.3% while single-family permits fell 1.2% in June. Both series have declined in four of the five months so far this year. Multi-family starts and permits, meanwhile, have been on a downward trajectory since early 2023. We look for multi-family starts to decline 1.5% in June but for permits to rise 2.9% after three months of even larger declines.

We think that initial claims for jobless benefits increased by 8,000 to 230,000 during the week ending July 13, partially reversing the large drop in the prior week. Claims data can and have been noisy around holiday weeks, and the incoming report might still have some noise after the Independence Day holiday. Initial claims have stayed relatively tame, despite some upward trend until last week. Given the gradual increase in the unemployment rate and some uptick in advance layoff data from WARN notices since March, we think a gradual further rise in initial claims might be likely as the labor market cools further. Note, however, that last year claims similarly trended up around the same time during the summer, but then later reversed course.

The June CPI report was very benign. The headline figure fell 0.06% (expected up 0.1%) and the core figure was up only 0.06% (expected up 0.2%). Within the core component, the long-awaited slowing in both tenants’ and owners’ equivalent rental inflation took place last month as the monthly increases skidded to low-side 0.3% increases after running around 0.4% increases much of the prior six months. Core services ex-rents slipped 0.05%, the second consecutive decline. Core goods prices slipped 0.1%. The report is, to use Powell’s words, “good news” and we now think this paves the way for a first cut in September (previously November), followed by quarterly cuts thereafter. Given that Powell has also said the decision to make the first cut will be “consequential” why shouldn’t we expect cuts at every meeting thereafter? We certainly think that’s an underappreciated risk, particularly if the labor market cools further. However, if the immaculate disinflation signal in the claims figures plays out, then the Fed should be able to take a more leisurely journey back to neutral.

In the details of the report, energy prices fell 2.0%, thanks in part to a 3.8% drop in gasoline prices. Food prices were up 0.2%, a little firmer than the recent trend. Tenants’ rent increased only 0.26% and owners’ equivalent rent was up 0.28%--both outturns the lowest since 2021. Given the signals in industry data about new rents, it is likely that much of this cooling in rental inflation will persist. In the core services exrent component, airfares slid 5.0% last month, though vehicle insurance prices increased 0.9% last month after a welcome decline in May. Medical care service inflation slowed to 0.2% last month. Within core goods, there were decline in both new (-0.2%) and used (-1.5%) vehicle prices in June.

In contrast with the downward surprise to the CPI report, the June producer price index (PPI) was firmer than expected. Headline producer prices rose 0.2% last month, while the core measure (excluding food and energy) rose 0.4%. There also were upward revisions to the May readings. Given these numbers, year-ago headline PPI inflation increased to 2.6% while core rose to 3.0%. Both of these are meaningful accelerations over May’s values and represent their highest readings in 15 months.

In the details, as expected energy prices were a drag on the month (-2.6%) while food prices were somewhat softer than expected (-0.3%). But higher margins boosted producer prices for services 0.6% in June, yielding an upside surprise for the monthly core print even as core goods PPI prices last month were flat. Based on this report, we now see the core PCE price index tracking a 0.17% rise for June, which if realized would keep the year-ago core PCE inflation rate at 2.6%.

Consumer sentiment (Jul 12)
<table><tr><td colspan="2">Michigan preliminary</td><td rowspan="2">Jun</td><td colspan="2">Jul</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>May</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Univ. of Mich. Index (nsa) Current conditions</td><td>69.1</td><td>68.2</td><td>70.0</td><td>66.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>69.6</td><td>65.9</td><td></td><td>64.1 67.2</td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>68.8</td><td>69.6</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td>3.0</td><td></td><td>2.9</td></tr><tr><td>Short-term</td><td>3.3</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.0</td><td></td><td>2.9</td></tr></table>

The preliminary July survey of consumer sentiment from the University of Michigan disappointed with a further slide to 66.0 from 68.2 for the June final release. While the headline sentiment measure has been trending higher since reaching its all-time low in the summer of 2022, it has declined in each of the past four months and now stands below its end-2023 level. Despite higher stock prices, lower gasoline prices, and the recent news of cooling CPI inflation, lingering dissatisfaction with the cumulative rise in prices since the pandemic appears to still weigh significantly on respondents. Indeed, the UMich survey remains far more negative than the Conference Board’s survey of consumer confidence, which appears to reflect a still-solid labor market than the weaker purchasing power of households. Within the preliminary July UMich survey, both current conditions and expectations moved down from their final June levels. The current conditions compo-

nent has been the weaker of the two more recently and now stands at a two-and-a-half year low.

Despite the survey director noting that nearly half of respondents spontaneously mentioned high prices eroding their standard of living (tying an all-time high), both 1-year and 5-to-10-year ahead consumer inflation expectations ticked down a tenth to 2.9% for each, suggesting expectations remain fairly well-contained. A special report from UMich researchers notes that a small share of respondents continue to offer extremely high long-run inflation expectations, biasing up the mean relative to the reported median measure. This behavior also was observed in the 1980s and into the early 1990s in the aftermath of the inflation surge years earlier. In addition, the now-completed methodological shift to online from telephone-based interviewing appears to have significantly biased up the mean level of longer-run inflation expectations, but not the median nor the broad stability of either inflation measure over time.

## Focus: S&L employment to stay firm, then fade in FY25

Employment in the government sector accounted for about one-third of the total increase in nonfarm payrolls last month. Even though the prior two prints reported slower gains in this sector, monthly government payroll gains averaged 31,000 over the past three months, slightly less than 20% of the total nonfarm payroll growth over that period (Figure 1). More broadly, over the past two years, the government was one of the main drivers of the overall employment gains along with other sectors like education and health and leisure and hospitality. We think the resilience in this sector is due in part to the catch-up hiring in state and local government education after the pandemic—some of which had been supported by education relief funds. It also can be attributed to healthy state budgets over the past couple of years. The most recent budgetary data point to continued support for state and local government hiring this year. But as these supporting factors all fade into next year, employment gains in this sector are expected to decline back to historical norms, to a pace slightly below 10,000 per month.

Figure 1: Payroll gains
![](images/2fa8a1f73ef6e8776fff5199d6c3330d785ee4bc04a82c42be6af80b972b0caa.jpg)

A significant portion of elementary and secondary school education is conducted at public institutions managed by state and local governments. As of June, for instance, employment in state and local government education was 10.7mn, about three times the level of private sector employment in education services. The pandemic hit educational institutions particularly hard, resulting in major dislocations with widespread school closures at every level. Early in the pandemic recovery, school reopenings led to a rebound in state and local education employment, which supported robust employment gains for the government sector overall. Over the most recent 12 months, average monthly gains in education-related government employment accounted for about one-third of the total monthly gains in the public sector (Figure 2). One potentially important source of support for state and local government education was the Elementary and Secondary School Emergency Relief (ESSER) funds appropriated under three

different pandemic-era legislations, which together provided about \$190bn to state educational agencies for elementary and secondary education relief. Most of the funding under ESSER and other education relief aid has since been spent by the states, although any remaining funds that are committed to specific purposes could be used through early 2025. Thus, an important fiscal support for education at the state and local level will be unwinding by early next year, although it may continue to offer some continued support in the near term.

Figure 2: Employment in public education
![](images/269136dbf92b0aa062a52970e047e3f67281b82dd6ef920750233d32119a18e4.jpg)

We think that another important factor for the underlying resilience in government employment over the recent past is due to relatively healthy state budgets. According to data from the National Association of State Budget Officers (NASBO), state budgets have increased by significant margins in each year since 2021. In fact, FY24 saw a record 13% increase. A few years earlier, there was an 8% increase for FY22—which, in turn, was the fastest pace since 1986. Figure 3 shows a decent—and arguably unsurprising—correlation between state and local employment growth and the lagged real increase in state budgets. With the record increase in 2024 noted above, this relationship points to the possibility that government employment growth might remain firm through mid-2025. However, significant headwinds lie on the horizon: while preliminary and subject to change, the latest report from NASBO forecasts state budgets collectively to contract 6.2% nominally in 2025—the largest such contraction on record.

Figure 3: State budgets and employment growth
![](images/aec67a62f58ee27bce3ecdf1b344d31c0772c8a6ff56602de85e5cd86ec9b6cb.jpg)

## Euro Area

• ECB on hold next week

• Confirmation that data are in line with staff forecast would signal a September cut

Data on wages consistent with ECB narrative of inflation catch-up this year and moderation next year

• Jump in household saving rate a puzzle and a challenge

Next week, the ECB is almost certain to keep rates on hold. The direction on rates remains downwards, but the ECB has signaled a skip in July and hence directed attention to the September meeting at which new staff forecasts will be available. As a result, the focus next week will be on the signaling during next week’s press conference. We suspect the signal will be pretty clear, even if in an implicit rather than explicit way.

The June minutes suggested that “some” members recognized that the data in the run-up to that meeting were not as supportive of a cut as the ECB’s prior signal had suggested. However, they still supported the move and there has been relief since then from the weaker June PMI and a smaller seasonally adjusted change in core prices in the flash June HICP report. Incoming news on wages is also broadly consistent with the idea that inflation-related catch-up is keeping wage growth high this year but that this momentum will moderate into next year. And while there are some other challenges, that was already the case ahead of the June meeting and did not prevent a rate cut. These challenges include the incoming core inflation data, which were a bit above the staff’s forecast in 1Q24 and were above it again in 2Q24. But, the Governing Council has shifted its focus to the broad disinflation narrative, which has remained intact, and the softer flash report for June likely helps as well.

As a result, we think that the ECB will view the incoming data as “broadly confirming” the assessment of the inflation outlook that is embedded in the June staff projections. This could be the most important part of the statement, for two reasons. First, to deliver the first cut in June, the ECB had to build up confidence in the disinflation the staff projections had been showing for a while. This was being assessed through Lagarde’s three tests. Hence, a repeat of the “broadly confirming” phrase would suggest that the three tests are still being met and the confidence in the baseline projection remains intact. Second, the baseline projection has further cuts factored in via technical assumptions they incorporate (i.e., market rate expectations from mid-May). Hence, implicitly that includes a September cut.

Hence, a confirmation of the June projections would implicitly keep a September cut as the base case as well. This appears consistent with recent comments from many Governing Council members, which have been favourable to another cut in September. Even for the hawks, such a move would keep restriction in place and is therefore seen as a relatively low risk move.

All of this will be balanced by the statements about “data dependence”, the “meeting-by-meeting approach” and that there is no “pre-commitment to a particular rate path”. That keeps some focus on the data flow over the summer, especially the two further PMI and HICP reports. But, as president Lagarde has said, ”data dependence does not mean data point dependence”. Hence, data surprises will only affect the rates decision if they materially shift the broader disinflation narrative via the three tests, including the staff projections. The ECB is prepared for some ups and downs in the data. In other words, while it took time for the data had to build confidence in the disinflation process before June, the situation has reversed and the hurdle is now likely higher for surprises in individual data points to dash that confidence.

## News on wages in line with ECB narrative

Incoming news on wages continues to be broadly in line with the ECB’s narrative that pay growth will remain high this year and moderate next year. Philip Lane has provided an update on the ECB’s Wage Tracker, which is still not being published (Figure 1). This includes the Forward Wage Tracker, which captures the implications of all existing deals for pay growth in the next few months. This shows that new wage information received so far this year has not pushed up the likely pay growth in 2H24. This remains at around 4.5%oya.

Figure 1: Euro area wage growth
![](images/6bd9e062b0f894dcbe46642dd270783f67e89b6d4c2488dee42e9d18da6515d8.jpg)

In terms of what is likely to happen next year, the Indeed Wage indicator from job postings picked up in June but to a large extent due to a narrowly-based jump in Italy. More importantly, our sense is that German pay deals are still following a structure that has large increases for this year followed by moderation next year. For example, a new deal for private banks saw pay rise 5.5% in the middle of this year, 3.0% in the middle of next year and 2.0% in mid-2026 (Figure 2). This profile is somewhat delayed when the implications for the calendar years are calculated, but that is only due to carry-over effects into the following year. Not all German deals have followed this pattern, but our sense is that the majority are doing so. This is important as a big part of the ECB’s disinflation narrative is that high wage growth still reflects catch-up to high past inflation, rather than the tightness of the labor market per se.

Figure 2: German pay deals for private banks %oya, orange bar shows the new deal for 2024-26
![](images/e4b64f03bc6c5850e11e49d481b20b75fedc32f3e09d12802dc88288c568a8af.jpg)

Information from the Netherlands is pointing in the same direction (Figure 3). The Dutch negotiated pay index that feeds into the ECB’s quarterly index of negotiated pay ticked up in June, but separate data that record only new deals continued to moderate. The latter have to be taken with caution as they simply average new deals and may not be representative of the sectors in the economy. But, the Dutch data are also consistent with some moderation being in the pipeline.

Figure 3: Netherlands negotiated pay
![](images/59c9d4b1a1346ed5359e5885ef6e855c8aef5a44cca180aeb340aa95cc7500ff.jpg)

## The Euro area saver puzzles

Recently released data from the sector accounts showed the Euro area household saving rate jumped from 14.1% in 4Q23 to 15.3% in 1Q24 (Figure 4). Combined with a household investment rate that continued to normalize lower, this seems to send a negative message about Euro area consumers. This is especially the case because the higher saving rate reflected a jump in real disposable income of 6.3%q/q saar in 1Q24. This reflects strength in labor income, which is most likely to be spent, rather than in other income components.

Figure 4: Euro area household saving and investment rates % of disposable income
![](images/c8807c7d3cde1ccf30181ba637fe73f3d34bb8bc38392b278090882708c0be6a.jpg)

Figure 5: Euro area households spending and income %q/q saar, 2qma
![](images/5aa16b52a1b23bb0779111b8c8b8039b931d9eed7a358974ec882c66e3cc6300.jpg)

There are few details to assess this properly. But, in Germany, real incomes were boosted by large lump-sum payments in a few pay deals (e.g. in the public sector) that were paid very late in the quarter. It is possible that the spending from this income boost will materialize only in the current quarter. The jump in disposable income was also very abrupt in Italy, which may reduce the likelihood of the income being spent immediately and this may explain the 3%-pt jump in the Italian saving rate. Nevertheless, that consumer spending grew modestly in 1Q24 and is not clearly accelerating in 2Q24 can be seen as somewhat disappointing.

Figure 6: Italy household spending and income
![](images/607adef28dbd8e15eda2858f011c5157292058e517461bc05cbe4bf8390c5739.jpg)

National reports point to declines in total IP and manufacturing output. This would leave total IP in Apr/May flat on 1Q24 and up 2%ar in manufacturing, in part due to support from Ireland. Excluding Ireland, the May data would be even weaker, but it seems that holiday-related effects were a drag in Germany, France and possibly elsewhere as well. That holiday timings played a role was mentioned explicitly by the French statistics office and, in Germany, we already know from the VDA that car production has been on a big roundtrip, rising sharply in April, falling sharply in May and recovering in June. This would leave the IP data running ahead of the PMI, which still points to an underlying trend of contraction. Due to this, we would still describe industry as weak overall.

The last ECB bank lending survey showed stable bank lending standards and a slower decline in loan demand. We suspect that these trends likely continued over the past three months. Banks’ risk perception may have improved further given the economic expansion and solid labor market. On the demand side, the weaker June PMI was likely after the sample period for the bank lending surveys, and therefore banks may report a further decline in the pace of loan demand contraction.

The flash June core estimate came on the soft side with a 0.2%m/m gain. This core price gain was weaker than seen in recent months. Indeed, core price gains averaged 0.29%m/m between January and May (with monthly volatility linked to the early timing of Easter cancelling out over this period). And looking at the details, the weaker core gain in June was driven by services (up 0.27% vs 0.44% on average between January and May) rather than core goods (up 0.11% vs 0.02% on average between January and May).

## Japan

Major banks requested the BoJ to significantly reduce its JGB purchases early at the meeting with the BoJ ...

… in order to restore market functionality and curb persistent expectations for higher yields

Incoming data suggest that domestic consumption is finally beginning to recover

• Full-time scheduled earnings marked a decades high gain, reflecting a part of the result of wage negotiation

The BoJ held a two-day Bond Market Participants Meeting this week, as announced in the June policy meeting. Although the BoJ did not provide clear plans for the pace of upcoming QT and the level of the BoJ discretion thereafter, the media reported that many participants indicated the desirable amount of the BoJ’s ultimate gross purchases to be around JPY3 trillion per month, halving from the current level. In particular, major banks and securities firms requested the BoJ to significantly reduce its purchases early in order to restore market functionality and curb persistent expectations for higher yields. While some other participants called for cautious reductions, the market appears to have begun pricing the possibility of a larger reduction in the BoJ’s JGB purchases compared to the expectations as of June. The BoJ appears to have succeeded in achieving the objective of making the market prepare for a “significant” reduction in the JGP purchases ahead of the policy meeting at the end of the month.

Incoming data suggest that domestic consumption, which had been sluggish for a year since 2Q23, is finally beginning to recover. Although total earnings for May fell short of expectations due to a significant decline in volatile special payments, underlying wages marked a decades-high gain, with full-time scheduled earnings rising 2.7% oya. As this only reflects a part of the strong result of spring wage negotiations, the trend rise in wages likely will continue for the coming months, reaching the mid-3% level by end-3Q. This, together with the one-off tax cuts starting from June, is gradually improving households’ income environment. To this end, we also received another positive sign for domestic consumption recovery, following last week’s improvement in consumer sentiment; SME’s business sentiment turned up in June, driven by household-related businesses. We expect GDP-based domestic consumption to finally break away from a year-long slump in 2Q.

We believe that these developments will support the BoJ’s policy rate hike in July. In response to the strong results of spring wage negotiations, the government is considering to raise the minimum wage by about 5%, a larger rise than last year’s 4.2%. Uncertainty still remains on the extent to which higher wages will push up underlying inflation. But strongerthan-expected wage momentum should increase the confidence of the BoJ in achieving the inflation target. In addition, elevated inflation expectations, strong wage momentum, and signs of a domestic consumption recovery point to a risk of inflation staying elevated for years ahead. The potential cost of postponing rate hikes is clearly rising for the BoJ, particularly as the bank has indicated its intention to avoid consecutive rate hikes even in the future.

## Rising momentum in underlying wages

Headline wages rose 1.9%oya in May, modestly weaker than our and consensus expectations of 2.2% and 2.1% increase, respectively. However, the May disappointment came from volatile special payment, falling 8.5%, and the underlying details look quite strong. Scheduled earnings, which exclude volatile special payments, rose to 2.5%oya, led by a sustained trend rise in full-time pay which marked a decades-high rise of 2.7%. Part-timers’ hourly earnings also continued to rise strongly, rising 4.0% in May (Figure 1). Constant sample data (which remove sampling volatility) also suggested strong wage growth of 2.3%oya from 1.8% in April.

We expect the base pay hikes agreed in the Shunto negotiations of the mid-3% levels to filter into the monthly wage data over the next couple of months, pushing average wage growth higher. Also, the negotiation over the minimum wage increase effective from October began a few weeks ago, and the government appears to seek a higher increase compared to the last year’s 4.2%oya rise to match with the strong result of the spring wage negotiations. With these moves, we expect that economy-wide earnings growth is likely to trend up to over 3% in the coming months.

Figure 1: Scheduled earnings composition %oya, both scales
![](images/33b8414dbcf9acf250a895daf2acb104e2e099f3e41a343fa5ef7f8e9405b51c.jpg)

While we expect inflation to remain higher than wage growth for the coming months, this strong wage momentum should be sufficient to pull average real wage growth into positive territory later this year. We have observed a sharp contraction in real wages over the past two years, due to lagged response of wages to inflation. However, if we are correct in judging that nominal wage growth has further to run, real income drags should continue to fade toward 4Q this year (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Wage and inflation
%oya, shaded period shows JPM forecasts
![](images/6c2b3b447140df18b9e653b34c17d5b5a6904874c93e3792aaa370bd09b09c04.jpg)

We think rising wage momentum will provide the BoJ sufficient confidence in hiking policy rate, albeit at a gradual pace. While the BoJ appears to have yet to gain full confidence that higher wages will translate into inflation, if wage inflation will start running over 3%oya, the BoJ needs to be prepared for the risk of inflation staying well above the target for long. If the BoJ prefers to avoid rapid rate hikes in the future, it likely will need to move early in raising the policy rates. We continue to expect the policy rate to be raised to 0.5% by end-2024, through two rate hikes in July and December.

## SMEs business sentiment turned up

Sentiment among small and medium enterprises (SMEs) rose in June, reversing the trend deterioration over the past months. The current conditions headline index rose 1.3pt to 47.0, the first monthly increase since February this year (Figure 3). The June improvement was mainly led by a large jump in the households-related business index, rising 2.1pt to 47.0, echoing the June rise in consumer sentiment. We have seen some positive signs in household consumption, possibly reflecting rising momentum for higher wages and income environment. However, the business-related index remained lackluster, falling 0.6pt to 47.3. The business-related SMEs indicated high uncertainty about the business environment with higher material and labor costs, as a reason for weak sentiment.

The outlook index sent a positive signal ahead. The headline index rose 1.6pt to 47.9, sending a signal for a further recovery. Both households and business indices rose, signalling rising SMEs business prospects. Although the employment index pointed to a further deterioration, this was due to the shortage of labor rather than weakening of labor demand. While SMEs are still struggling with higher cost pressures, we take today’s report as an encouraging signal for upcoming economic recovery, albeit at a modest pace.

Figure3: Economy Watchers Index, current conditions and outlook DI, sa
![](images/19b1b2bd2bd2c31587ebe7d46a476ae613f55131cf8f9a48c5aa7e41ffc9cdc8.jpg)

## Upside risks to capex in 2Q

May core machinery orders declined by 3.2%m/m sa, marking the second consecutive quarterly decline. However, these declines came after significant gains in March, resulting in a run rate of 18.2%3m/3m saar (Figure 4). With the trend rise in manufactures’ orders since the start of the year (Figure 5), we see some upside risks to our forecast for a 3%q/q saar gain in GDP-based capex in 2Q.

## Canada

• Markets are pricing in a BoC cut in July...

• ...but we see the Bank proceeding with more caution

• We forecast headline CPI eased to 2.6%oya in June, while core held at 2.9%oya

• May building permits pared back April gains; existing home sales rose for the first time in five months

With less than two weeks until the next Bank of Canada policy meeting, there were few data of import out this week—but next week should be more impactful. Data on existing home sales and building permits this week suggested the housing market is still chopping along with a shortage of supply in some high-demand locations. Looming on the horizon is next week’s June CPI report. The Bank was comfortable kicking off its easing cycle early last month as its preferred core inflation measures eased into the target zone, but the subsequent May CPI report surprised to the upside. For June, we project headline CPI eased to 2.6%oya—led by a drop in energy prices—while core CPI moved sideways at 2.9%oya.

That may not be quite enough progress for the Bank to feel comfortable cutting rates at consecutive meetings, particularly after Governor Macklem emphasized in a speech late last month that subsequent easing will proceed gradually. We interpret that remark to suggest the Bank is not necessarily looking to ease in back-to-back meetings—although it remains data dependent—as it does not want to risk jeopardizing the progress to date toward price stability.

Markets are now pricing over a 70% chance the BoC will cut again later this month, which seems to put much more emphasis on the soft June Labor Force Survey than the hot May CPI report. Absent measurable progress on core inflation next week, we see the Bank instead remaining on hold. Our forecast does not have the BoC easing again until 4Q, but the risks for a September cut are rising, particularly with the Fed now expected to start its own easing cycle then. For the BoC to even consider cutting in June, the sizable downside surprises seen in recent US CPI inflation reports would have to migrate north of the border next week.

## Housing market hopes

Housing activity has stabilized after a sizable boom post-pandemic; Figure 1 shows that existing home sales have settled at a level around if slightly below their pre-pandemic average. Zooming in to the recent data from the Canadian Real Estate Association, existing home sales rebounded 3.7% in June for the first increase in five months. Higher interest rates by the BoC were expected to take some of the steam out of housing demand, and Figure 1 suggests that did indeed happen during

2022. Mortgage rates are now starting to move back down as the BoC embarks on its easing cycle, but the gradual and data-dependent policy stance has led to a limited decline thus far, to levels still above where they were a year ago and well above their pre-pandemic range. We expect June housing starts will have slid 3.6% in next week’s report—another sign that tight policy is still weighing on the housing market.

Figure 1: Home sales and mortgage rates
![](images/111933131a2dabe20a0043a78ac0ea871a211fb8d73b037c1db7ec708f0d2f84.jpg)

What the housing market needs is additional supply, but data on building permits do not point to clear signs of improvement there either. While the nominal value of permits has been rising since the pandemic began, in real terms the trend appears to have been gradually lower—although if one squints perhaps there are signs of a rebound in recent months. Still, total building permits on a constant dollar basis fell 12.5% in May, with a sizable decline in British Columbia unwinding an unusually large April gain. Excluding the volatile British Columbia data, the total value of building permits dipped just 0.7% in May—nonetheless making it hard to get excited about any potential upward trend. While a drop in Vancouver contributed to the overall decline, the number of permits issued in the perennially hot housing market of Toronto edged up further in May. Both residential (-16.3%) and non-residential (-5.0%) permits declined in May, with a notable slump (-22.3%) in the volatile multi-unit component of the residential sector. Multi-unit residences are an important piece in addressing tight housing supply.

Figure 2: Total value of building permits
![](images/8dac3d590d163aabc6b0c94b4cc92079bc9fe3bc3e509fe11d757ca22555044e.jpg)
Source: Statistics Canada, J.P. Morgan

## Mexico

• Banxico dovish pivot reinforced in meeting minutes

• We still expect a 25bp cut in Aug; Odds of Sep cut rise

• Inflation spiked on food but core is still easing

• IP rose as expected but manufacturing is still weak

The minute of Banxico’s latest policy meeting confirmed what the post-meeting statement hinted at: the board pivoted dovishly and was on the watch for additional evidence that it is “prudent” to continue with gradual rate cuts. Parsing through the individual board member commentary, our take is that there were three members already leaning dovishly (one, DG Mejía, voted for a rate cut in that meeting) and two hawkish members who continue to advocate a harder line when it comes to the degree of progress in the battle to bring inflation back to target.

This latter splits the two groups apart in very broad terms. Dovish members think that, yes, a tight stance (the real rate is currently around 7.25%) is needed, but not as tight as in the past, as inflation has come down a long way. We share this view. The most recent inflation print showed core prices up 4.08%oya (a whisker away from 4%, the upper bound of the tolerance range), 3.6%3m/3m, saar, and 3.2%3m, saar. This latter reading is unsurprising, since core price-gains have been just below their historical norm (0.29%) in the three months through June. Also, dovish members put more weight on the GDP slowdown in the past few quarters than on financial markets turbulence post-election.

We think that data since the decision have generally gone the way of the dovish members. As said, core inflation continued to gradually grind lower, with services in particular improving (one of the hawkish board members pointed to these two measures as concerning, so should be somewhat appeased). That said, DG Heath has now expressed concern publicly about a food-driven spike in headline inflation. We think that even with that concern in mind, the core narrative will dominate.

Against this backdrop, we think that the next decision (August 8) will bring about a 25bp rate cut, and feel reassured about it. The more benign external backdrop (i.e., markets more aggressive pricing of cuts by the Fed) also helps, even if indirectly through a stronger MXN. At the very least it should be a 4-1 decision, but might well end up being a unanimous call. We think risks are now split for a cut in September (we forecast only one more 25bp cut in December this year), and incoming data and political developments will be key to tilt one way or the other. Before we thought of September as a low probability event.

## Core falling in line in spite of headline spike

As we expected the June CPI brought with it a stark divide between headline and core trends. Headline prices surged 0.53%2w/2w in the second half of the month, and sent the year-over-year marginal rate to an uncomfortable 5.2%, well above the upper bound of the target range. However, out of the 53bp increase in prices, 33bp came from raw food, and an additional 9bp from energy. By contrast, core added merely 10bp to the headline print.

In a recent note we argued that core disinflation had slowed but remained on track, and that at a compositional level it had turned for the good, with services prices starting to post smaller increases. This week’s report reinforces this view. Core prices rose just 0.13%2w/2w in the second half of June, whereas we looked for a 0.17% increase. Furthermore, goods prices were a little firmer, and the bulk of the downside came from services (though from tourism to a large extent). On the month as a whole, core prices were up less than their historical norm (0.295%m/m, sa) for the third consecutive month, or the second straight month if we remove the volatile Mar/Apr readings.

The core inflation picture has improved by any metric. Going from trend to momentum: the over year-ago rate stood at 4.08%oya, the 3m/3m annualized rate at 3.6%, and the 3m annualized rate at just 3.2%. Furthermore, all major subcomponents (save housing) fell below 4% (the upper bound of the tolerance range) for the first time since early 2019.

Figure 1: Core disinflation %3m, saar
![](images/278c57900e9d410854e4902c60184324bfacc38ec6575fdba65d3c47faf04db4.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan

Finally, the all-important non-tourism category (“other services” less tourism) continued to rapidly moderate its pace of price gains, hitting 0.19%m/m, sa in the month, the lowest in over two years. The associated 3m annualized rate of change dropped to 4.2%. Our core diffusion index also has shown a marked improvement recently.

## IP on and off

IP growth in May was in line with what we expected, 0.7%m/ m, but the composition was not. Manufacturing disappointed once again, extending this frustrating stretch of fits and starts that do not evolve into a full takeoff in this sector, which has been plagued for the past year and a half, almost two (Figure 2). Worth noting, the Statistical lnstitute (INEGI) seasonally adjusts the aggregate series instead of building the aggregate as the weighted average of seasonal adjusted subseries, most of which grew solidly in the month. Looking at the details, 16 of 21 industries expanded and the weighted average yielded overall manufacturing output growth of 0.6%m/m. Still, INEGI reported flat output.

Be that as it may, the seasonal adjustment methodology from INEGI has not changed recently so we were not puzzled by our contrasting results either. In any case, this week’s release was not that surprising given the divergent trends between manufacturing and construction on the back of opposing dynamics in external and domestic demand. Overall, factory activity is tracking yet another drop in 2Q.

Figure 2: Construction and manufacturing production %m/m, sa
![](images/9b34279c61f3021f1d8061abe3a39191eafa79a4c3c20ef2e991159215b12102.jpg)

On the bright side, concerns about a sudden death in construction must at least be suspended for a quarter. Construction output slid at the turn of the year after an impressive run, but seems to have retaken it. Output grew 1.4%m/m, 2%, and 2.5% in March, April, and May, setting it on track for a 17% annualized surge last quarter. Government spending lent a helping hand, but it will take some time for us to see just to what extent (data details on the breakdown take at least one more month).

Mining posted a surprisingly strong 2% gain, but the sector is still struggling with stagnant oil production. Overall IP is tracking close to a 2% annualized gain in 2Q, keeping in place some downside risks to our 3.5% GDP growth forecast for that quarter. Utilities also rose a sturdy 3.5%m/m, leaving the series at its highest point since December 2020.

## Brazil

• Floods in Rio Grande do Sul were a significant hit to economic growth

• However, there is underlying strength beyond our expectations

• We thus see upside risks to our GDP growth estimate for 2Q

Core services CPI was back within the target range in June

We estimated that the floods in Brazil’s southernmost state (Rio Grande do Sul) halved GDP growth this year by 0.1% to 0.3%-pt, particularly in 2Q with weakened economic activity in May. As a result, by the time of the release of 1Q GDP, we cut our 2Q GDP growth forecast to 1%q/q, saar, but expecting the reconstruction effort to lead the economy to rebound in the second half of the year. Economic growth indeed seems to have been hit by about this magnitude, with retail sales falling almost 3% and industrial production down 26% in the Rio Grande do Sul state.

However, the fall in goods sales and production in Rio Grande do Sul was offset by other factors. Despite the decline in goods consumption in the state, elsewhere across the country retail sales have strengthened. In fact, when removing methodological changes introduced in 2023 that made the headline sales data harder to read, we find that sales have grown every month this year. Nationwide manufacturing production decreased 2.2%, led by the slump in Rio Grande do Sul, but the bounce back of the mining sector—following a sequence of contractions at the beginning of the year—partially compensated for it and industrial production decreased only 0.9% in the month. Finally, the services output for May was better than expected, remaining stable, though these data were distorted by an abnormally low deflator that led to a surprising upswing of services in the Rio Grande do Sul state during the flood period (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Activity indicators in May
![](images/29cb045db46a26e4246b03081e239e59e3726415ad78fd989d959407e7b517ea.jpg)
Source: IBGE and J.P. Morgan estimates

Not only were the May data stronger than anticipated, but more timely coincident data for June also suggest a significant rebound in the sectors affected by the floods. New vehicle sales and production, which respectively declined 15% and 32% in May, were back up 17% and 57% in June. In addition, heavy vehicles road flows decreased 4.4% in May and rebounded almost 6% in June, returning to its trend after the initial shock in May. Sentiment indicators remain solid as well. All of this kept our nowcaster suggesting upside risks to our 2Q GDP estimate (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Brazil 2Q nowcaster
![](images/e987a0ce42f0e6ee29fc2b547b114c8bd1ae8b04f90f6d3717191a287af94e26.jpg)

This economic growth resilience even in face of a significant and concentrated shock suggest better-than-expected underlying strength of the Brazilian economy. The recent data then reinforce our view that Brazil was well positioned to grow this year, given the health of private-sector balance sheets. In this context, odds are that this year’s GDP can surprise and again grow beyond our above-consensus call for a 2.2%y/y expansion.

## Core services within the target range in Jun

There was good news in the June CPI report, though there are still areas to track. The June CPI came out at 0.21%m/m, nsa, 11bp lower than our estimate of 0.32% and the consensus estimate of 0.3. The surprise was concentrated in food and core goods prices, with services roughly in line with expectations. The result drove the headline three-tenths higher to 4.2% but left core CPI unchanged at 3.5%oya.

The details of core CPI show a services-led slowdown, close to our expectations, but with a significant downside surprise coming from goods impacting core CPI. Core CPI grew the equivalent of 2.8%m/m, saar—the second lowest figure of the last 12months—though the somewhat higher May figure drove the 3-month average to 3.2%, from around 3% in the last couple of months . This means that by any metric core CPI is now running between 3% and 3.5%, close to the midpoint of BCB’s target of 3% (Figure 3) . The rise of core goods CPI was much more muted than anticipated, but this line still accelerated to 1.6%3m/3m, saar.

![](images/ccfe2aab5fe98e248573367a4797e54e0785535068bbaa8803562d707a044ae1.jpg)

The all-important core services increased 3.9%m/m, saar, close to the lows of the last 12 months as well and after being around 5% for most of this year, core services decreased to 4.5%3m/3m, saar and 4.4%oya, back within the target range. Despite these improvements in many parts of the services CPI, the labor-intensive services CPI remains sticky running around 5.5% (Figure 4).

This week’s important surprise with June CPI would likely reduce our expectations for the July CPI as well. However, the government announced a gasoline price hike that likely raises the CPI prospects by 15bp this year, with two-thirds of the impact coming in July. As a result, we only slightly raised our July inflation estimate to 0.36%m/m nsa, a CPI that should be affected by temporarily higher electricity tariffs as well. We keep forecasting CPI to end the year at 4%, expecting a more challenging second half of the year given the FX depreciation.

Figure 4: Seasonally adjusted core services CPI metrics %3m/3m saar, black lines show the target range
![](images/19d86e0b9ebffa89f6b751f10634567447ea19557525c6ca6e516cbd6b0a4a8e.jpg)

## Argentina

Headline inflation came in at 4.6%m/m in June (271.5%oya); core inflation proved sticky

• We expect Dec-24 inflation at 138%oya

Releasing capital controls is a necessary condition to consolidate a sustainable disinflation path ahead

• May construction gained, but manufacturing and mining slipped

Early June activity data: Mixed construction, auto production down but local sales up

## Core inflation proved sticky

Headline CPI posted a 4.6%m/m increase in June (271.5%oya and 79.8% YTD), below our expectations (4.9%) and market consensus (5.2%). The monthly inflation print would thus mark a slight rebound from the 4.2%m/m reported back in May, on the back of regulated prices adjustments. When adjusting for seasonality (J.P. Morgan’s adjustment), national headline CPI gained 5.1%m/m, sa, with the last-three-month sequential pace easing to 107%, saar.

As anticipated, core CPI proved sticky to the downside at 3.7%m/m, in line with prior month, yet below the 6.5% monthly average in the prior three months (Figure 1). Meanwhile, our preferred metric of underlying inflation (adjusting core inflation by excluding food prices) printed at 4.1%m/m (4.7%m/m, when sa by J.P. Morgan), accelerating from the prior month. Worth noting, the seasonal adjustment shows ex. food core momentum at 86.6% in annualized terms, decelerating on base effects from 122.5% the previous month.

Figure 1: Headline and core National-CPI
![](images/46b4b4a43702ee4fc15ec7a149f92501d6ebc71208005872b7690e7b56d6f762.jpg)

As noted above, regulated price increases explained headline inflation’s positive differential versus underlying inflation. Indeed, regulated prices increased 8.1%m/m (1.5%-pt contribution) in June, given the increase in electricity tariffs, gas, subway, and pre-paid medical services. Meanwhile, seasonal prices decelerated further to 4.4%m/m (0.5%-pt contribution), from 7.2%m/m (0.8%-pt) last month.

The government announced it will postpone the scheduled electricity and gas increases for July, as well as the increase in fuels tax. In our estimates, such adjustments jointly added 1.2%-pts to headline CPI in July. While the future pace of regulated price adjustments remains uncertain, we assume regulated price increases are partially resumed in October. We estimate around 5%-pts in additional contribution to headline CPI coming from increases in electricity, gas, fuels, and transportation prices.

Against this backdrop, we revise lower our 3Q24 inflation average to 4.5%m/m (from 5%, previously), with a modest rebound entertained in 4Q24 (5% monthly average), assuming the transition into a new policy framework by year-end. This is consistent with year-end 2024 inflation forecast at 138%oya. Worth noting, we see monthly core inflation averaging 3.6% in 2H24, a level that will be difficult to break absent further amendments to the policy framework, unless a stronger domestic demand contraction transpires than the one expected. Advancing toward promptly reducing the (still large) relative price misalignment and releasing capital controls are two conditions we view as necessary to consolidate a sustainable disinflation path ahead.

## Mixed signals from early June data

The INDEC sector data for May proved to be a mixed bag. On the positive side, construction showed signs of life , up by 6.2%m/m, sa, the second consecutive monthly gain. Despite the decent monthly performance, construction remained 31% below the level observed back in Oct’23.

On the other side, manufacturing slipped by 0.2%m/m, sa, with the 3-month sequential momentum converging to -14.8%ar. That compares with -27% in 1Q24. Therefore, manufacturing production is poised to converge close to -10%ar in the second quarter, and is expected to stabilize in the third quarter. The other weak print came for mining, down by 0.8%m/m, sa. Different from other activity variables, mining production enjoyed a very strong first quarter of the year (+19.8%ar), so the sequential correction in the second quarter is roughly aligned with our base case. For the full quarter we expect a 3.6%ar contraction, also regaining positive momentum in the third quarter.

Following the real tax collection data discussed last week (see note), the June series for auto production and local sales were reported, alongside cement sales and a private indicator for construction activity. The auto production series showed a significant decline in the second quarter, down at a 38% annualized rate (ar), following a 29% ar drop in the first quarter. Overall production is now 42% below the peak observed last year, with no indications of a near-term recovery.

However, domestic auto sales were a bright spot, increasing by 3.6% month-over-month (m/m), seasonally adjusted (sa), recovering by about 15% compared with April’s lows. Domestic auto sales often serve as a useful forward-looking indicator for overall consumption, a trend echoed by the behavior of the UTDT leading index. This performance, along with gains in pensions and real wages later in the second quarter, aligns with our base case for improved sequential consumption performance in the months ahead.

Construction data presented a mixed picture, with cement sales down by 8.9%m/m, sa, while the private indicator Construya was up by 9.7% on the month (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Early month activity indicators levels, Oct-23=100, seasonal adjusted
![](images/f2aa1c4b8c9a3b5ecbfc5994dcf986095766dcf37c1c2957b6c9798f7e990906.jpg)
Source INDEC, sectoral chambers and J.P. Morgan

In summary, the marginal data align with our base case for GDP stabilization in the second quarter of the year, following the declines observed in the prior two quarters. More importantly, the performance of consumption-related variables suggests that consumption will moderately bounce back in the third quarter. That said, policy decisions in the second half of the year, including the removal of the capital controls, will be crucial in determining whether the expected recovery extends into next year, as we currently foresee in our base case. Our annual GDP forecasts for 2024 and 2025 remain at -3.4% year-over-year (y/y) (consistent with -5.4% for ex-agriculture GDP) and +5.0% y/y, respectively.

## Andeans

Chile: June’24 CPI logged -0.12%m/m, but annual inflation accelerated to 3.8%oya

• We project 2024 year-end headline CPI at 4.4%oya, above the CBC’s central scenario (4.2%oya)

• Colombia: Both annual and sequential inflation proved stable in 2Q24

We revised 2024 inflation higher to 5.4%oya, on firmer food prices and the recent currency weakness

• Our base case is for a new 50bp policy rate cut at the upcoming meeting at the end of July, to 10.75%

## Chile: CPI roughly in line with expectations

In a nutshell, June CPI showed moderate deflation, explained by seasonality (Cyberday) and gasoline and some transportation prices. However, the impact of the normalization of electricity prices after being frozen for five years (and debt close to 2% of GDP) is becoming evident and should continue to exert upward pressure on inflation, as the CBC recently acknowledged (see note). Our forecast for July stands at 0.65%m/m. The adverse inflationary dynamics, along with additional factors, are likely to limit further monetary easing. We expect only one more 25bp policy rate cut for the remainder of the year.

The details show that headline CPI logged -0.12 %m/m in June. Despite the monthly deflation, the last 12-m inflation (referential CBC splice) converged to 3.75%oya. When adjusting for seasonality (J.P. Morgan’s sa adjustment), headline CPI logged 0.3%m/m, sa, with the last 3-month annualized momentum converging to 4.1% ar, above the ceiling of the inflation target range (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Chile headline CPI
![](images/3d7d746f78f9c9644b681353a5043185454531842e3e2787771d83d7cbb67135.jpg)

Moving to underlying inflation, ex. volatile CPI recorded -0.20 % on the month, with the annual rate easing by a whisker to 3.2%oya. When adjusted for seasonality, core logged +0.1%m/m, sa, with the last 3-m annualized pace at 3.3%ar. Our base case is for sequential core CPI to accelerate, averaging close to 4% annualized in 3Q, as sequential core services should accelerate and core goods should normalize.

In terms of forecasts, we project the 2024 year-end headline CPI at 4.4 %oya, above the latest CBC central scenario revision (4.2%oya). Of note, we had estimated about a 120bp contribution from electricity prices through the end of next year, acknowledging the prevailing uncertainty also with regard to the timing of the adjustments within the next 12 months.

## Colombia: Inflation stuck in high gear in 2Q

The Jun’24 monthly CPI printed 0.32%m/m, so annual inflation remained relatively stable at 7.18%oya. When adjusting for seasonality, headline CPI logged 0.67%m/m, sa—which brought the 3-month annualized pace to 6.96%, still well above the target range ceiling. In the same vein as annual inflation, sequential momentum printed stable in 2Q, with the gap between the two series almost disappearing. Looking ahead, both sequential and annual inflation are expected to resume a downward trend in the coming months, enabling the central bank to continue gradually easing monetary conditions.

Regarding underlying inflation, core CPI as tracked by the BanRep (ex-food & regulated) gained 0.30%m/m, and on a 12-month basis rose by a whisker, to 6.0%oya. Equally relevant, the seasonal adjustment showed a monthly pace of 0.49%m/m, sa, with the last 3m, saar pace running at 5.5% on an annualized basis. Likely the silver lining is that core services continued to gradually ease, though to a still high 7.0%ar. We do project further deceleration ahead, but to levels well above pre-pandemic averages (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Colombia CPI
![](images/230b3293876fc1770097460ffdef417dc68b8501b7ea3471e491d176e4151834.jpg)

In terms of the CPI forecast, the firmer food prices observed in June, together with the currency weakness observed in June impacting producer prices prompt a mild revision higher for headline CPI ahead, up to 5.4%oya by year-end, a tad below the BanRep central scenario (see note).

CPI

## Review of past week’s data

## BanRep minutes align with a new 50bp cut

The minutes of the latest policy meeting noted the consensus among all members to continue with the process of reducing the intervention interest rate that began in December of last year, which is deemed as consistent with the downward trend that annual inflation has maintained since April 2023. All directors valued the government’s recent efforts on the fiscal front, with a notable adjustment in spending compared to 2023.

Among the prevailing risks that prevent a more aggressive easing pace, the Board emphasized the downward rigidity of inflation in some services, and the depreciation of the peso against the dollar in recent weeks. They also highlighted the risk related to food prices, which saw a rebound in inflation in May. Additionally, they warn that although inflation expectations have shown a downward trend over time, they remain above the target for the policy horizon.

On monetary policy, our base case is for a new 50bp policy rate cut at the upcoming meeting scheduled for the end of this month, to 10.75%. We project the policy rate level for Dec-24 at 8.5%, a level highly dependent on inflation breaking the inertia experienced in 2Q. On the terminal rate for the cycle, we maintain the level at 6.25%, due to a higher r\* than what BanRep estimates due to fiscal risk (see note for discussion).

## United Kingdom

• May GDP surprises to the upside

• Looking for a moderation in year-ago inflation for June next week...

• … setting the BoE up for an August cut

Core inflation indicators remain strong, but the BoE has a bias to ease. We expect it to cut in August provided next week’s June CPI avoids a significant upside surprise in services inflation. We look for over year ago services inflation to slow to 5.4%. GDP this week for May rose 0.4%m/m, suggesting upside risks around our forecast for 2% annualized in 2Q.

## Strong May GDP, upside risk to 2Q forecast

Monthly GDP (Figure 1) surprised to the upside in May, rising four-tenths on the month (1.4%oya). As flagged in last week’s Data Watch, there was upside risk to construction output owing to weather-related factors (the UK experienced its warmest May on record, in a series going back to 1884). Construction soared 1.9%m/m sa, well above consensus expectations of 1.0%. Industrial production rebounded 0.2%m/m after a large decline in April, while services posted another solid gain of 0.3% over the month (consensus: 0.2%). The latter was the largest contributor to GDP (0.24%-pt). Consumerfacing services rebounded 0.7%m/m after a 0.5% contraction in April (3m3m%, saar: 1.9%). Non-consumer-facing services, which tends to be less volatile, rose two-tenths on the month and is now up by 5.2% annualised on a 3m/3m basis. This suggests that business-related activity is supporting the expansion, even as the consumer recovery remains somewhat tepid. But, with real income gains remaining strong, we continue to expect the consumer to do better as well.

Figure 1: Monthly GDP
![](images/59801ee8dfbc3683ed9b6ee21dd6a1fd2298e5ac6c2eaa11777a43afe860c68d.jpg)

Overall, the services sector has now shown five consecutive monthly output gains. Likewise, GDP itself appears to have broken out of a monthly cycle of small gains followed by contractions. GDP is now running 0.6% above its first-quarter average, indicating upside risk to our and the BoE’s 2Q forecast of 2.0%q/q, saar. If delivered, it would mark a second consecutive upside surprise for the Bank (1Q GDP came in at 2.5% vs. its 1.4% projection).

## Preview of next week’s CPI

We expect a decline in headline inflation from 2.0% to 1.9%oya in next week’s June CPI, with core inflation down from 3.5% to 3.4%. We forecast services inflation to fall fall back from 5.7% to 5.4% reflecting a partial unwind of June’s spike in transport services prices. There may be some upside risk from the Taylor Swift concerts that occurred in the UK during June and had a large impact on Swedish inflation for May. But our best attempt at identifying the sample dates used to collect prices of potentially affected services (e.g. accommodation) suggests the UK may have managed to dodge a very large influence (Figure 2).

There may have been a lingering impact on prices away form the main concert dates. But Sweden also had to deal with Eurovision at the same time, and probably has less capacity than the UK to absorb large demand shocks from a concentrated stampede of tourists. We also note there was no clear evidence of an impact on services prices around the time of the 2021 euro finals in London and the 2012 Olympics. There may also be scope for a larger downward adjustment in transport prices than we have allowed for.

Source: Wikipedia, ONS, SCB

Our forecast would leave services inflation still running three tenths above the 5.1% forecast made by the BoE in May. However, most of this miss came from the April surprise, which the MPC played down at the June meeting. And then the May surprise was more narrowly concentrated in transport. The MPC has been placing more emphasis on survey measures of pricing lately, and we noted last week that the DMP wage expectations reading continued to move in a more moderate direction. A large genuine surprise in underlying services inflation (e.g. excluding transport) would probably be needed to cast doubt around our call for an August CPI cut.

## Sweden and Norway

• Clear downside surprises in June Scandi inflation...

• ...leading to the Riksbank cutting at next two meetings

• ...and Norges Bank in December, earlier than guided

The Swedish activity data for May was generally disappointing. While monthly GDP rose 0.1%m/m (sa), this came on the back of a sharp 0.7% contraction in April. If activity is steady in June, this would leave 2Q GDP down 3.4%q/q, saar, well below our (-1.0%) and the Riksbank’s forecast (+0.1%). Turning to private sector production,the headline index rebounded 0.6%m/m after a 0.5% contraction in April, driven by an increase in industry and services. In contrast, construction plummeted 2.1% after, conversely, increasing 1.3% in April. While the rise in industry was encouraging, the average in April-May sits 2.3% below the 1Q average, conflicting with the more upbeat PMI and NIER survey.

Swedish services PMI rose 2pts to 51.8 in June, which coupled with a small step back in manufacturing PMI left the composite index at 52.3. The 2Q average is consistent with GDP up a modest 0.2%q/q, saar, which is more downbeat than the NIER survey (+0.6%). In 2H24 we think growth should turn positive, with rising real wages underpinning private consumption. On a sector level, we expect the industry to improve, with the manufacturing PMI rising to 54.0 in July (Figure 1). The services PMI should, on the other hand, correct a bit lower, leaving the composite index close to 52.

Core prices fell 0.1%m/m in June, with the year-ago rate falling 0.7%-pt to 2.3%oya. This was below expectations, making an August cut close to a done deal. There was a partial reversal of the Swiftflation impact in May, which according to our calculation had contributed 0.28%-pt to the monthly rate. In response to the soft June report, downside risk to the bank’s 2Q GDP forecast, and our new Fed call of a cut in September, we have tweaked our Riksbank forecast to include cuts at the two upcoming meetings in August and September. We do, however, still look for a total of three cuts in 2H24.

Figure 1: Sweden: PMI manufacturing model
![](images/9f6dab0cee393e2887fa1f8dfff7cde18c75327e72fa787b8f75cd0cd8aa7dd1.jpg)

## Norway: Another CPI downside surprise

Core inflation rose 0.23%m/m, nsa, in June with the year-ago rate falling 0.7%-pt to 3.4%oya. This was two-tenths below expectations, and a continuation of the year-long trend of downside surprises to Norges Bank’s forecast and consensus. Significant downside risk to inflation vs. Norges Bank’s forecast was our highest conviction call in our 2024 outlook, and remains a key reason why we expect a cut in December (vs. forward guidance of 1Q25).

When there are large surprises to core inflation, food prices are the usual suspect. That was not the case this time, however, as these were quite strong (1.2%m/m, sa). Instead, services prices were very weak (-0.25%m/m, sa) following a strong reading in May. This was the fourth-lowest reading on record (data from 2015). Headline inflation landed at 2.6%oya, the lowest in 3.5 years and 0.5%-pt below Norges Bank forecast (but in line with our call).

Ahead of the June data we had projected core inflation to start deviating substantially from Norges Bank’s new forecast by July (0.4%-pt). We now think the gap will widen to 0.5%-pt (Figure 2). Although we expect services prices to rebound slightly in July, strong food prices in June suggest hikes will not be as aggressive in July (as assumed by Norges Bank). While food prices historically would soar in July in connection with the semi-annual negotiations between suppliers, this pattern has changed over the past year as prices are now smoothed out. On net, we have raised our m/m forecast for July by one-tenth to 0.7%m/m (nsa), leaving the year-ago rate at 3.2% (Norges Bank: 3.7%).

Otherwise, there are few data releases in coming weeks. We expect house prices to be soft in July (0.1%), after a strong start to the year. The sales-to-inventory indicator highlights a risk of decline on the month. Finally, we expect the registered unemployment rate to hold steady at 2.0%, while the manufacturing PMI should recover after a large fall in June.

Figure 2: Norges Bank core inflation forecast history
![](images/9d765338ca1464818c3912d08a437a69e18ef6cdc28343d2f3a63e975584bffe.jpg)

## Emerging Europe

• Hungary CPI: weak food and energy mask sticky core

• Czech CPI: Large downside miss

• Israel BoI: Firmly on hold, for now

• Romania: NBR starts shallow easing cycle

## Hungary CPI: weak volatiles, sticky core

The June inflation print marks a continuation of recent data, with very benign pricing dynamics in food and energy disguising inertia in core inflation. Headline inflation fell to 3.7%oya in June, from 4%, marginally lower than expected by markets (J.P. Morgan: 3.8%oya, Consensus: 3.9%). Inflation has now been within the NBH’s target tolerance band (3%oya +/-1%-pt) for six consecutive months, and may actually remain so for most of this year (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Hungary inflation
![](images/deecb2d5409b99d47b6cc3f28c79ec05542e526b0fb0879c1e4254795b84d84b.jpg)

Food prices remain weak, falling 0.7%m/m in June to -2.9%oya (from -1.9%), driven mainly by raw food items which fell 1.8%m/m (vegetables in particular were down -8.1%m/m), whereas processed food prices were flat. In momentum terms, food prices are falling at an annualized pace of around 3.5%. Energy prices were also very soft, with natural gas prices falling by 4.6%m/m in June (a methodological construct reflecting lower consumption, as tariffs are fixed) and fuel prices down 3.4%m/m (more than suggested by the surveys: -2.3%), reflecting Brent price moves.

Unlike food and energy, pricing in the core categories remained firm into June. The NBH’s main core CPI metric (CPI ex. raw food, alcohol, tobacco, energy, regulated prices and adjusted for indirect tax changes) was up 0.5%m/m, leading to a small increase in over-year-ago terms to 4.1%oya (from 4%). The alternative measure, which we prefer, which also excludes processed food from the basket, was up 0.6%m/ m (0.4% expected), resulting in a flat 5.6%oya (we expected 5.4%), the first month it hadn’t fallen in the last 14.

Core inflation went through a long period of genuine disinflation between 2Q23 and the first few months of 2024. During that period, core momentum collapsed, paving the way for a slower, but steady decline in the over-year-ago rate. However, in the last four months, core CPI momentum stagnated at elevated levels and, in this context, the over-year-ago only kept declining only due to the favorable base, but that is now over. Over the past four months, momentum has run above or at the same level as the over-year-ago rate, suggesting an exhaustion in the core disinflation process.

In the details, the upside strength in core CPI was driven mainly by tradables this time around, which were up 0.5%m/ m or 0.3%m/m sa (3.6% annualized), the highest this year. This is in part due to classification issues, as one key driver of the upside was package holidays, which fall inside the NBH tradables categorization, whereas in countries that use a goods/services split, this item falls on the services side. On the non-tradables side, there were no major surprises, prices were up 0.6%m/m or 0.77% sa (9.6% annualized), still extremely elevated, but close to our expectations.

We expect the effect of low energy and food to disguise the resilience in underlying inflation through most of this year, with headline CPI fluctuating between 3-4%oya most of this year (Figure 2), possibly jumping above target only in December. Core inflation, however, is unlikely to do as well; we expect core (ex. processed food) will fall only marginally between now and year-end to 5.3%oya (from 5.6% currently), slowing further next year to 4.5-5%, assuming the type of backward looking price indexation we saw early this year in telecommunications and insurance does not take place again.

Figure 2: Hungary inflation forecast
![](images/94da6761843d2e53290e9b58e4983403c0bbe1ed2031f4fce12a03fb38ab21b4.jpg)

The takeaway from the CPI print for monetary policy is that there is evidence of exhaustion in the disinflation process and hence it pays to be cautious. Together with relatively a high EUR/HUF the data increases the chances of the NBH keeping rates on hold this month. At the same time, rates in Hungary remain relatively high by regional standards and the NBH targets headline CPI, not core, so it may take comfort in the past six months inside the target band, and continue easing. We see still some room for easing this year, and expect three rate cuts of 25bps (to 6.25%), the first being this month. Risks are, however skewed for a pause, and much will depend on the EUR/HUF moves between now and the NBH meeting date (July 23).

## Czech CPI: Large downside miss

In June, Czech inflation surprised on the downside, with prices down 0.3%m/m, which resulted in the over-year-ago falling to 2%oya, from 2.6% (Consensus: 2.4%oya, J.P. Morgan 2.5%). Non-core items contributed significantly to the surprise, but unlike in Hungary, we cannot claim that is the full story. Here the downside miss is rather widespread, encompassing food, energy, goods and services (Figure 3).

![](images/32fc048a07f282dd8b154bac86bf891ca3c3f3ea1a349ab2b9ab9a3174032745.jpg)

Food prices came in much weaker than expected, dropping 0.6%m/m (a move that was driven by almost all sub-categories), whereas we expected a 0.5% increase and the midmonth surveys pointed to even more. Alcohol and tobacco likewise posted a big downward move, falling 0.6%m/m (+0.6%m/m expected). Fuel prices fell 3.7%m/m, roughly as indicated in the weekly surveys and hence weak, but unsurprising. Regulated prices were roughly unchanged. The combination of low monthly prints and base effects resulted in declines in over-year-ago terms across all non-core categories.

Unlike in Hungary, the Czech downside surprise is widespread and includes essentially every sub segment of the basket. This includes core inflation which fell to 2.2%oya, from 2.5%, much lower than our expectation for a flat reading. June is generally a month of stronger inflation as it tends to include a jump in package holiday prices, due to the approaching school summer holidays, but this time printed extremely low, at levels compared to the 2016-2017 low inflation period. In part, this is because the usual move in package holidays did not occur: prices for travellers were up just 0.2%m/m, compared to 8.7%m/m June last year or 11.9%m/m in 2022.

This year, the typical seasonal behavior of package holidays has been distorted so it makes sense to single out developments in this category. But this time around, even abstracting from holidays, core prices came in quite benignly (Figure 4). We estimate that in seasonally adjusted terms, core ex. rents and holidays was up 0.17%m/m sa, which is around 2.1% in annualized terms, a second consecutive month virtually at target.

![](images/5800270412fc96c7c526b89b0b0a85d52f266603d9a4d9d4a09691e395e23366.jpg)

If it persists, low core momentum could allow the CNB to cut deeper. At its last meeting, the CNB sent a quite forceful signal that in coming meetings the pace of easing was likely to slow to 25bps or, in the extreme, even a pause could be considered. The latest data, however (beyond all the one-offs and oddities which could bring a payback next month) is clearly benign and suggest the next forecast could include lower guidance for mid-term rates. The currency has been weak, and is tracking around 2% weaker than the CNB forecast, but we think the downside surprises to inflation are significantly larger than the upside risks stemming from FX weakness. The possibility of another 50bp cut in August is back on the table, but is not the most likely outcome in our view, as the CNB already has the lowest rate in the region and is already in sub-Fed territory. If the current low momentum is confirmed, we think rather than cutting more forcefully in the near term, the board could instead aim for a somewhat lower year-end rate. For now, we maintain our call for the pace of cuts shifting lower to 25bps in August, and the policy rate ending the year at 4%.

## Israel BoI: Firmly on hold, for now

The BoI kept the policy rate unchanged at 4.5%. The decision was widely anticipated, but a significantly more hawkish messaging was arguably a bit of a surprise. The BoI now appears more firmly on hold in the near term and signals only one 25bp cut on a one-year horizon. One reason is, apparently, a more sober assessment of war risks — the BoI staff now assume a higher intensity war in Gaza to span into 2025. This implies elevated risk premia, supply constraints and related inflation pressures for longer, hence less space for easing. The inflation forecasts for 2024 and 2025 were raised by a cumulative 0.8%pts and GDP growth lowered by 1.3%-pts. Yet, not all of the downward GDP revisions were due to a downbeat

view about the supply side — demand is also seen to be weaker, with the 2025 unemployment rate now 0.5%-pt higher than in the previous forecast.

Our forecast remains somewhat more dovish than the prevailing market view and, now, the BoI’s messaging. In particular, our inflation forecasts appear to be somewhat more benign — in contrast to the BoI, we do not anticipate CPI to breach the upper limit of the target range (3.0%) in coming months, and our mid-2025 inflation forecast is 0.3%-pt below that of the BoI (2.9 vs 3.2). Our preference therefore is to keep two cuts in our forecasted profile for the BoI, but the MPC’s current leanings leave much less space for action in coming months, which is why we push the timing of the next expected 25bp cut to November, from October previously.

## Romania: NBR starts shallow easing cycle

After refusing to cut in May on an inflation forecast revised higher and upside risks to the inflation outlook, National Bank of Romania (NBR) was surprised strongly to the downside in both the April and May CPI releases. Consequently, last Friday, the bank cut the policy rate by 25bp to 6.75%, largely in line with consensus and our forecast. The corridor remained unchanged, basically implying a cut in the deposit facility to 5.75% and in the lending rate to 7.75%. There were no changes to reserve requirements – cutting those for RON looks highly unlikely given the surplus of RON liquidity.

As normal for NBR, there is no forward guidance in the statement, but some further easing is implied. The statement now mentions that inflation is on a “significantly lower path”, suggesting more easing may follow. We forecast another 25bp rate cut in November and could see that brought forward even to August (we indicated timing of rate cuts is less clear). There is even a small risk of two more rate cuts delivered this year – our baseline is for only one additional rate cut to 6.50%, but a lower level of 6.25% cannot be ruled out. Such cuts would bring the effective policy rate to 5.25% or lower, significantly below Poland at 5.75%.

We remain of the view that space for rate cuts is severely constrained. NBR is still concerned with the medium-term inflation picture as core inflation momentum is elevated – it is the most elevated in CE4. Thus, its easing cycle cannot be deep and certainly less pronounced than in CE3. The other big constraining factor is linked to the extremely poor fiscal performance so far in 2024 and the fiscal adjustment needed post elections. The latter will likely include tax hikes, including value added tax, and could prove highly inflationary. If tax hikes are delivered, NBR might refrain from rate cuts in 2025 (we currently have a baseline of 100bp). This would put NBR is a similar place to NBP which is hawkish because of prospective regulated price increases. In the past NBR mentioned

NBP as a benchmark – delayed NBP cuts (or even no cuts until 2026 as stated by Governor Glapinski) could certainly have an impact on NBR too.
