## South Africa

SARB likely to keep the policy rate unchanged at 8.25%

We look for a 25bp cut in November, but see rising odds of a September move

● High frequency IP data unexpectedly contracted in May

• Full quarter tracking still mixed as manufacturing output holds up

The near-term inflation outlook has moderately improved, but the SARB will likely err on the side of caution and keep the policy rate at 8.25% next week (Figure 1). While we retain our call for a hold, a discussion around the likely start of an easing cycle probably will feature, with a possibility of a dissent in the MPC vote. In our view, the MPC will juggle three key developments: inflation dynamics, the global backdrop, and the impact on the ZAR from recent political outcomes.

While the SARB is unlikely to be in a hurry to begin the cutting cycle this month, we believe the case for near-term easing is building. Domestic inflation developments are broadly dovish for the policy rate outlook with core trending closer to the 4.5% midpoint target, while ZAR has broadly cheered local political developments. The SARB’s 2H24 inflation projection could be marked down by 0.1%-0.2%pts to average 4.9%oya (from the 5.3% recorded in 1Q24). This should take the full-year inflation average to 5% (from 5.1%; JPMe: 4.9%). Also of relevance is the economic growth outlook and country risk premium (fiscal outlook). Our base case is for a first policy rate cut in November, with about a 30-40% chance of a September move.

Figure 1: South Africa inflation outlook
![](images/9d683a49a6c7926c3c2587139e8fa1f5e210f864b27cff9c060edced7991c66a.jpg)

## Mixed 2Q IP data

High frequency activity data into mid 2Q do not give clarity on the strength of GDP recovery. Mining is tracking a 2.7%q/ q saar contraction for the quarter as output declined by 0.6%m/m sa in May (from +0.8% in April). PGMs and gold had the largest weighted drags with an 11%m/m and 4.6%

fall. Manufacturing output also fell sharply by 3.2%m/m saar, but because April was strong (+5.2%), production still tracks a 6.6%q/q saar gain (from -5.7% in 1Q24). The weak monthly prints come even as we have not witnessed power cuts since April, and terms of trade generally trending higher in May. This highlights the breath of industrial activity drag from the lack of reforms and logistical bottlenecks. All told, we now see downside risks to our 2.5%q/q ar GDP growth bounce in 2Q, unless June data deliver significant upsides.

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australian new home lending declined in May, matching softness in other housing market indicators

• We anticipate Australia’s jobless rate increased to 4.1% in the June survey

• The RBNZ pivoted dovishly in this week’s meeting, the August meeting looks live for a cut

• We expect NZ 2Q CPI rose 0.4%q/q nsa, two-tenths below the RBNZ’s forecast

In a light week for data in Australia, the ABS reported that the value of new housing loan commitments fell 1.7%m/m in May, weaker than we (+1%m/m) and consensus (+1.8%m/m) had anticipated. The fall reflected declines in both the value of owner-occupier (-2%m/m) and investor (-1.3%m/m) loans. The new loan series had been running very strongly through the first half of the year, and the average growth rate over the past three months is still above 2%m/m. In trend terms, the series increased in May and the value of new loan commitments remains commensurate with housing credit growth of around 0.4%m/m.

Figure 1: House price growth and auction clearance rates
![](images/6d7b3b578238192b2bba50b4ce3478d87111f22e94d28d496c6fae61613130fd.jpg)

Still, this week’s declines are in keeping with softening in some of the other housing indicators. Both auction clearance rates (Figure 1) and sales turnover, which are a good leading indicators of house price growth, have eased and suggest dwelling price growth will decline in 2H24. The value of new owner-occupier loan commitments tends to follow house price growth reasonably closely, so we expect owner-occupier loan growth will soften relative to the heady pace at the start of the year. Investor loan growth may be a bit more resilient, with tailwinds still coming from elevated rental yields and greater borrowing capacity compared to owner-occupiers.

## Jobless rate holding firm

We expect Australia’s unemployment rate increased to 4.1% sa in June, alongside a steady participation rate and a 20K gain in employment. The jobless rate declined a tenth in the May survey, but this mainly reflected unwind from distortions in the April data related to changing seasonality. Looking through the monthly volatility, the jobless rate has been about 0.3%-pt higher on average across 1H24 compared to 2H23. The unemployment rate is still low in historical terms, but roughly in line with the full employment jobless rate implied by the Beveridge curve and close to Treasury and RBA assessments of NAIRU.

Figure 2: Beveridge curve when labor market loosening Vacancy rate, %
![](images/3dd0a11366a722ea60f382d7609e0a2eec1bbcdd6083c76272a5e41f8761e574.jpg)

If our forecasts for June are broadly correct, the jobless rate will have risen a little under 0.7%-pt from cycle lows in 4Q22. Like other DM labor markets, the gradual rise in the unemployment rate mainly owes to the fact that the economy has been operating on a steep portion of the Beveridge curve (Figure 2). With labor demand very elevated, firms have moderated demand for new hires, rather than reducing existing headcount, keeping both the separation rate and unemployment rate low. The latest data suggest that the labor market is moving toward a flatter segment of the Beveridge curve (Figure 2, again), so further declines in the vacancy rate should generate larger increases in unemployment. This dynamic might explain the RBA Board’s observation that, “the unemployment rate could rise quickly once it did start to rise,” in the June minutes.

## RBNZ pivot dovishly

While keeping the OCR on hold at 5.50% as widely expected, the RBNZ shifted distinctly dovish this week. The short-form MPR statement (no MPS/forecasts, no press conference) flagged that cuts are coming, and showed more willingness to act on the basis of the path of %q/q inflation, rather than waiting for base effects to roll off and fully reflect achievement of the target band in %oya rates.

As of the staff’s last published forecasts in May, the bank has been signalling achievement of the target band on %oya inflation in the 3Q24 data, but no cuts until mid-25. That combination seemed implausible, so had to change at some point, but the timing has been uncertain.

The RBNZ has a history of non-linear guidance shifts, which the Bank itself describes with analogy to the white heron ,

New Zealand

Week of July 15-19

whose “flight is seen but once… once ready, open your wings and commit to flight”. The preference for sudden pivots rather than gradualism follows from the RBNZ’s need to control financial conditions (front-end swap rates) which drive mortgage pricing. It also means that the gap between changes in message and in the policy rate is typically very short, so this week’s signal makes August live for a first cut, per our longheld call.

The imminence of the potential easing is underscored by the statement title “inflation approaching target range”, and the committee seems to now have greater confidence as "there are signs inflation persistence will ease”. The opening line in the statement is written in a backward-looking sense, seemingly declaring success “restrictive monetary policy has significantly reduced consumer price inflation”.

Though far from a lock, we keep our call for a first cut in August. We have expected cuts to proceed linearly to begin with, assuming an end-24 level of 4.75%. We look for the cash rate to reach 4% in 2H25, as this would still imply a real ex post cash rate of nearly +2%. Arguments for a materially higher long-run neutral rate in NZ compared to pre-pandemic don’t stack up in our view, given the performance of the economy under higher rates and as the new macro-prudential infrastructure (DTI restrictions introduced in May) should mute several inflationary externalities from lower rates that operate through the housing market.

## NZ 2Q CPI tracking 0.4%q/q nsa

This week’s NZ selected price indices indicated that, for 2Q as a whole, food, fuel and accommodation imply a deceleration %q/q nsa inflation relative to 1Q. Taking the monthly tracking together with other correlates from the business surveys (e.g. last week’s very soft outcomes on pricing intentions, costs, and high readings on labour availability in the QSBO survey) puts our forecast for next week’s full 2Q CPI result at +0.4%q/q nsa. The RBNZ’s standing forecast is +0.6%q/q.

The distribution of CPI items has looked quite ‘normal’ for a while now on a %q/q ar basis, with the share of right tail items sitting at 2019 levels, and the number of items annualizing in the target band at its historical average. Core inflation has also been annualizing in the band for a few quarters, particularly if we exclude the impact of lapsing subsidies in 3Q last year, which are still holding up the %oya rate.

## Greater China

• China: June CPI inflation eased modestly; general pricing power weakness will not easily go away

• June export activity showed steady growth; imports disappointed

• Further slowing in June credit and money supply growth

• FX reserves fell US\$9.7bn to \$3,222.4bn, largely in line with expectation

• Taiwan: June trade activity beat expectation; revising up GDP growth trajectory

China’s June CPI inflation rate eased modestly to 0.2%oya (0.0% m/m sa, Figure 1). The overall pricing environment remains rather weak: taking the first six months together, headline CPI inflation has averaged 0.1%oya. Core CPI inflation also remained soft, rising 0.6%oya in June (or 0.0% m/m sa). Food prices appear to be stabilizing (with recent uptick in pork prices), while energy prices have moved along with global commodity prices, with volatility in travel and transport costs along with holiday seasons. Aside from these factors, general pricing power for goods and services seems to have remained soft amid sluggish domestic demand growth and weak private sector confidence.

Figure 1: China CPI trends
![](images/818a8940ac66607bf714ec314abe0aba3566af05f44225e012377fdbda4a9715.jpg)

PPI deflation eased modestly in June, as headline PPI fell 0.8%oya (vs. -1.4%oya in May). In sequential terms, PPI edged up 0.1% m/m sa in June. Details suggest mixed trends in prices of industrial metals and energy-related PPI, while PPI for auto/NEV continued to fall.

Overall, we expect the low inflation environment to stay through 2H24. A steady outlook on global commodity prices and moderate recovery in domestic pork prices, along with low-base effect from last year, would help to avoid the return of CPI deflation in 2H24. Meanwhile, demand-supply imbalance and excess capacity concerns remain significant. Our forecast for full-year 2024 CPI inflation stands at 0.4%. Together with prolonged (though moderating) PPI deflation, the GDP deflator will likely stay in deflation through 2Q-3Q.

## Export activity showed steady growth

China’s June exports continued the solid growth trend (Figure 2), with a modestly higher-than-expected growth rate at 8.6%oya, translating to an increase of 1.3%m/m sa (or 4.8% 3m/3m saar). By product breakdown, exports of low-end consumer goods softened, down 2.6% 3m3/m saar in June. Exports of mechanical & electrical products and high-tech products have trended up in recent months, rising 10.5% 3m/3m saar and 14.6% 3m/3m saar respectively in June. Auto exports eased, down 6.7% m/m sa in June and 5.2% m/m sa in May. By destination, exports to DM markets ticked up in June, including exports to the US (+1.9% m/m sa) and Europe (2.3% m/m sa). Exports to EM Asia edged up 0.6% m/m sa, with mixed trends in exports to other EM markets (Russia: +2.8%m/m sa, LatAm: -1.5%).

Figure 2: China merchandise exports and imports
![](images/99f905fed0501e172709f68030d3fd0179c65b19bfdc0771abb76372b8181b54.jpg)

Nominal imports disappointed in June, falling 2.3%oya, or contracting 2.9% m/m sa. Breakdown by source of imports suggests underperformance in imports from EM Asia (-2.8%m/m sa), especially from Taiwan (-6.0% m/m sa), as well as imports from Japan (-7.9% m/m sa). Imports from other DM economies showed solid gains, including the US (+7.2%) and EU (+3.6%). Imports of commodities seemed rather mixed, with a modest rise in imports of crude oil (1.3%m/m sa) and coal (5.5%). Regarding imports of major industrial metals, imports of copper slowed notably (down 12.0%m/m sa), while imports of iron ore ticked up 2.3% m/m sa. Imports of mechanical and electrical products stayed flat (0.0% m/m sa), and imports of high-tech products rose modestly by 0.6%m/m sa. Overall, domestic demand softness remains a near-term growth concern.

The trade surplus registered US\$99 bn in June and \$436.3bn in 1H24, both the highest on record. The up-trend in global manufacturing activity amid improving global final demand, along with strength of Chinese exports (the scale of production and lower export prices), jointly lifted China’s export volume by about 12%oya (average for Jan-May). China’s share of world exports has risen to 14.7%, sa in 1Q24 (vs.

average 14.2% in 2023). Chinese exporters’ diversification efforts across products and destinations may continue to support export performance in the near term. But the rise of protectionism, the wider use of tariffs, 301 investigations, export controls, investment restrictions and sanctions, may lead to further distortion or disruption to the global trade system and exert pressure on macro growth.

## Credit growth slowed further

June credit growth slowed further, following the large disappointment in April and modest uptick in May. TSF flow came in at 3,298 billion yuan in June, and TSF growth eased further to a new record low of 8.1%oya (Figure 3). Bank loan growth also slowed further to 8.8%oya. M2 growth slowed to 6.2%oya in June, while M1 decelerated further, falling 5.0%oya (another record low). Regarding the key credit components, household loans picked up moderately in June, possibly reflecting modest sequential improvement in home sales. Medium and long term loans to corporates ticked up in June (in line with regular seasonal pattern), likely suggesting ongoing industry policy support. On the fiscal side, net government bond issuance eased modestly, after the significant pickup in May. Shadow credit continued to contract.

Figure 3: China TSF growth
![](images/9d4a0602647531276a45207ad889c34cec20ad577cb0a6b78089b4c8d60cbcf7.jpg)

The general softness in recent credit data not only reflects weak credit demand, but also raises concerns of credit supply constraints, as banks’ NIM pressure and slower profit trend restrain their ability to support loan growth and digest bad debt. We expect credit growth to stay subdued for the rest of the year, with TSF growth at 8.1% and loan growth at 8.4% by the year-end. Our forecast looks for one 10bp policy rate cut in the current quarter (likely in September), which is also the only cut in 2024. The positive news of softening inflation in the US and a repricing of first Fed rate cut to September open the door for the PBOC to shift focus from currency stability to domestic issues.

## FX reserves largely in line with expectation

China’s June FX reserves fell US\$9.7bn to \$3,222.4bn (Figure 4), largely in line with our expectation of \$3,225.8bn. It follows the US\$31.2bn rise in May. For 1H as a whole, FX reserves fell US\$15.6bn. Same as May, gold reserves stayed unchanged, after solid increases in the prior 18 consecutive months. Meanwhile, the US dollar index ticked up from 104.7 to 105.9 in June, leading to an estimated currency valuation loss of US\$15.6bn. Taken together, we tentatively estimate a US\$33.4bn implied capital outflow in June (vs. -US\$22.0bn in May).

Figure 4: China monthly change in FX reserves
![](images/6073523d7f07874f1b9d38edbf0ab320dc0188a342bb8fc504da1143e0d86dba.jpg)

Foreign investors added another US\$24.0bn holding of Chinese bonds in May, led by \$12.2bn inflows for NCDs and \$7.1bn for policy bank bonds. June foreign bond flows data is not published yet. Our strategists’ tracking suggests a return of retail bond outflows in June after May’s inflows, though at a moderate pace. We expect foreign bond inflows may have continued in June, but at a slower pace than May. For equity flows, Stock Connects recorded another US\$17.3bn net outflows in June (vs. US\$7.7bn in May), the largest net outflow in the past 9 months. In further breakdown, we had \$6.1bn outflows via Northbound Connect, and \$11.1bn outflows via Southbound Connect.

## Taiwan: trade activity beat expectations

Taiwan’s June trade report came in significantly above expectations, as merchandise exports grew 23.5%oya. Seasonallyadjusted, we estimate that exports (in volume terms) rebounded 5.7% m/m sa in June, following the decline of 2.1% m/m sa in May (Figure 5). Meanwhile, nominal imports grew 33.9%oya in June, following the modest increase of 0.6%oya in May. Overall tech exports rose 5.7% m/m sa in June, including a solid gain in exports of information and communication products (reflecting AI-related exports), which increased 15.0% m/m sa. Non-tech exports rose 4.9% m/m sa in June. Following the generally sluggish trend since 2Q22, machinery exports have picked up some momentum lately, rising 7.7% m/m sa in June (up 20.0% 3m/3m saar), consistent with constructive global capex trends.

Haibin Zhu (852) 2800-7039 haibin.zhu@jpmorgan.com JPMorgan Chase Bank, N.A., Hong Kong Branch Grace Ng (852) 2800-7002 grace.h.ng@jpmorgan.com

Ji Yan (852) 2800-7673 ji.yan@jpmorgan.com

Figure 5: Taiwan nominal and real exports
![](images/dbccd2b891f80559ea9fdd58dde27ebc3eb8587e9c61b512fcfbf53b4a81db10.jpg)

Going forward, our baseline forecasts continue to look for a steady uptrend in Taiwan’s tech exports in the coming quarters, with ongoing structural strength in AI-related demand, along with a gradual recovery in other tech demand areas, including PC, smartphone, and other consumer-related tech demand. Overall, recent signals from Taiwan’s activity data have consistently come in on the positive side, highlighting solid near-term momentum in external demand and industrial activity. Meanwhile, upward momentum in the external sector seems to be feeding to the domestic front, as total employment picks up some momentum lately, along with an uptick in retail sales.

## Revising up GDP growth trajectory

Recent signals from Taiwan’s macro data have consistently come in on the positive side, including the June trade report, manufacturing PMI, along with strong readings on May IP and export orders reports. The constructive external sector activity has fed to the domestic front, with an uptick in employment growth across industrial and service sectors, and a pickup in retail sales. Also, amid the solid external demand outlook, domestic capex appears to have picked up momentum in recent months. Taking these factors into consideration, we have revised up Taiwan’s 2Q GDP forecast to 4.5% q/q saar (previously: 2.3% q/q saar), to be followed by steady 2H growth at around a 2.6% q/q saar pace. Overall, our full-year 2024 growth forecast has been revised up to 4.7%yoy (previously: 4.3%yoy).

## Korea

BoK pushed back on near-term policy rate cut expectations

## • Unemployment rate unchanged in June

The BoK maintained its base policy rate at 3.5% at its July meeting as widely expected, yet the overall communication had a hawkish bias compared with the market expectation of a near-term rate cut. The MPC official statement and the BoK’s assessment of CPI inflation suggested a gradual increase in the likelihood of a policy rate cut. Nonetheless, the detailed communication underscored its cautious approach, adhering to a 'Festina Lente' strategy to balance the risks of premature and delayed rate cuts. Consequently, we reaffirm our expectation that the BoK is not in a rush to cut the policy rate in August. Instead, it is likely to wait a couple more months before initiating the rate cut cycle.

## BoK sounded hawkish in July meeting

There were a few changes in the BoK’s communication to suggest a gradual rise in the likelihood of a policy rate cut. The official MPC statement included a clause that it will consider a rate cut, the BoK now sees a downside risk to its annual CPI inflation forecast made in May with a welcome June CPI print, and the number of MPC members open to a rate cut (within three months) has increased from one to two, out of the total six members.

However, these dovish signals were not sufficient to outweigh the BoK's hawkish reservations. The current BoK dot plot does not actively suggest a near-term policy rate cut as a majority of the MPC expects a 3.5% policy rate three months ahead. Also, the July decision was unanimous as the potential dissent vote was delayed (likely to August, in our view). Importantly, the governor mentioned that “the market's expectations for a rate cut seem somewhat excessive”. The BoK is also now placing more weight on financial stability concerns, including household leverage growth and potential FX market uncertainties, according to the governor’s press conference.

As we have emphasized before, the BoK views the risk of a premature rate cut as greater than that of a delayed one, at least in the near term. Considering the balance between the stabilizing inflation trend and the persistent risk of household debt, we believe that further confirmation of inflation stability in the July and August data would prompt the BoK to cut the policy rate in 4Q24 (more likely in October than November) to partially ease monetary policy tightness. However, the subsequent rate cut cycle is likely to be shallow (we anticipate a total cut of 75bp by the end of 2025), given the BoK’s vigilance regarding household leverage risk, which is a structural issue that cannot be resolved in the short term.

## Labor market cools only gradually

Labor market conditions were broadly unchanged in June, while the details suggest a gradual slowing of employment and labor force growth. The seasonally adjusted jobless rate was unchanged at 2.8% in June for the fourth month, and employment stood flat in June after falling 0.4%m/m, sa in May (Figure 1). Labor force fell 0.1%m/m, sa in June after falling 0.4% in May. As a result, both employment and labor force (as a share of population over age 15) continued to moderate; the employment rate recorded was 62.6% compared with 63.0% in February, and labor force participation ratio fell to 64.4% from 64.6% in April.

Figure 1: Korea labor market
![](images/88679ae9607bb8a8e90fb6017bbc84721e4204ad0626580f3020761e008082c3.jpg)
Source: NSO, and J.P. Morgan

Our long-term call on the labor market details were maintained: Service sector employment growth is led by IT and transportation instead of traditional consumer-facing services, and the construction sector is struggling. Construction employment fell 1.0%m/m, sa for the fifth consecutive monthly fall, hospitality service employment fell 1.4% in June after recovering for three months, and distributional service employment edged up 0.2% but after falling for four months. Meanwhile, IT service employment rose 2.4% and financial service employment rose 2.3% (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Korea employment by sector
![](images/77095fabf62e52b117d92449005dcf4d37b053760e7ff0077907f5e022eed112.jpg)

While employment growth is slowing, overall labor market conditions are stable with a rotation of growth drivers as new types of service employment buffer the drags in consumer service employment. We continue to expect that private consumption growth will slow further this year as a whole, but the degree of this deceleration is anticipated to be gradual.

## ASEAN

Thaliand’s near-term constructive outlook remains intact despite weak May activity data

Government intends to finance digital wallet scheme via official budget and state-owned policy bank loans

• Legal issues and political uncertainty remain key risks to wallet scheme implementation

Despite Thailand’s weak May activity data, we think that the near-term cyclical lift is intact, driven by manufacturing recovery, the catch-up in fiscal spending, and steadily improving domestic demand. The government has revised up FY24 and FY25 budget deficits in preparation for the digital wallet scheme. Although there is ample system-wide liquidity, there are legal issues concerning acquiring funding from a state-owned policy bank, in addition to political uncertainty.

## Stay the course

Our pivot to a more constructive growth outlook was somewhat challenged by tepid economic activity data in May. Manufacturing production index (MPI) unexpectedly contracted 1.5%oya (consensus: 1.4%oya) while private consumption (1.2%oya) and private investment (-3.0%oya) indicators also pared gains from the previous month.

Nonetheless, we think there are reasons to fade the weak May activity data. Given the complete post-pandemic normalization in services activity, the GDP outlook once again hinges on the manufacturing sector (Figure 1). In this regard, we note that the factory output recovery has become more broadbased (Figure 2), catalyzed by the upturn in the regional tech cycle and a healthier inventory profile. A stronger-than-expected recovery in Chinese domestic demand is also an upside risk.

Figure 1: Thailand mfg output vs. real GDP
![](images/ed6897113f4805edccc52aa884526cf8cd41b6b93f398bfe55edfaf864c7eb18.jpg)

Figure 2: Thailand manufacturing production index (MPI) Dec19=100, sa
![](images/8772e6c3df4a6955301b44a75cc7810a7dad850949e6e85ad701e4dc0c624995.jpg)

Domestic demand is also on the mend, driven by backloaded FY24 fiscal spending and the faster-than-expected normalization in Chinese tourist arrivals (scheduled flight as at mid-July: 81% of 2019 level), with the digital wallet scheme posing as a significant upside risk to growth in 4Q24 and 1Q25. However, there is a reasonable degree of sectoral variation. Within private consumption, staples and services have outperformed discretionary spending (Figure 3) as bank lending standards tighten in the face of the household debt overhang. Within private investment, segments that are leveraged to global trade (e.g., capital goods imports; Figure 4) are doing better although the belated fiscal capex could turn infrastructure-related construction around in the near term.

Figure 3: Thailand private consumption indicator (PCI)
![](images/e3be7b4946fc20ac909fbc729dc0638506777de4cca719fff23bdac380f9a61a.jpg)

Figure 4: Private investment indicator (PII) components 4Q19=100, sa
![](images/868b739af40c1ff7ea81215b974a412001747acb6a5085745fea5fc3fef682af.jpg)

All told, we maintain our 2024 GDP estimate of 2.7%, with growth momentum climbing from 1.5%oya in 1Q24 to a peak of 3.9%oya in 4Q24 (partially from base effects).

## Wallet scheme financing faces scrutiny

Since coming into power, the Pheu Thai-led government has had to go back to the drawing board multiple times to address the legality of issuing an emergency bill to finance the digital wallet scheme (THB500bn or 2.5% of GDP). In the latest iteration (see link), the government proposed to fund THB330bn (or two-thirds) of the scheme from the official state budget, with the remaining THB170bn via loans from the Bank for Agriculture and Agricultural Cooperatives (BAAC), a state-owned policy bank. As a result, the FY24 and FY25 budget deficit have been revised up to 4.3% and 4.5% of GDP respectively, from a previous baseline of 3.7% of GDP, pushing up the public debt ratio as well (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Thailand fiscal balance vs. public deb % of GDP
![](images/14efaeaa5a24603630855c1b95aee9efd178dcedc376e4809a9dd829130d3813.jpg)

While this funding proposal removes the need to justify offbudget borrowing on emergency grounds, there are questions on whether supporting the wallet scheme is in line with BAAC’s legal mandate. On top of that, specialized financial institutions (SFIs) such as BAAC tend to have weaker balance sheets (Figure 6) and liquidity buffers, partially due to the accumulation of delayed reimbursements from the state.

On the other hand, given the ample system-wide excess liquidity due to weaker loans formation (Figure 7), we think that commercial banks will have little issues financing the rest of the THB330bn spending, whether via promissory notes or bond issuances. In the past year, domestic banks have been the marginal buyer of bonds as foreign funds and even domestic institutions pare back support (Figure 8).

As of now, the funding structure of the wallet scheme is going through a vetting process at the State Council and other legislative processes. The pending court cases concerning PM Srettha and the Move Forward Party also add to the political and policy uncertainty.

Figure 6: Thailand commercial banks vs. BAAC Equity-to-asset ratio, %
![](images/7ab6c6a1213fde557fb7dca992112b371fd9a707024b6f72aea36a7e797a9893.jpg)

Figure 7: Thailand excess liquidity
![](images/c45536e8300d6258c44fd4bb8f846081a16878f092f194fc296100a309972736.jpg)

Figure 8: Thai GB ownership by entity
![](images/64a6c53fb4ea6fbe833374c2a4d6d546123876b5da8b7bd1622df334c3012cd8.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan estimates

## India

• Volatile vegetables push up CPI inflation

• Core prices remain soft

• IP rise in May bucking the heatwave

• July rains narrow monsoon deficit

June CPI came out higher than expected due to a spike in vegetable prices driven by a deficient June monsoon and an extended heatwave. While prices of food ex vegetables also increased, the outlook has improved after a pickup in rainfall in July and improving sowing patterns.

May IP printed stronger than expected on the back of anticipated strength in electricity and mining as well as stronger than anticipated manufacturing bucking expectations of a heatwave-driven weakness.

## Vegetables drive up CPI

We had recently marked up our June CPI forecast from 4.4% oya to 4.9% on surging vegetable prices. In the event, the actual print was even stronger than expectations coming in at 5.1% (J.P. Morgan: 4.9%; Consensus: 4.8%) with vegetable prices (+14.2% m/m, sa) accounting for most of the surprise (Figure 1) . This dynamic has continued into July, and it typi cally takes 2-3 months for vegetable price shocks to reverse.

Figure 1: Food inflation
![](images/ff65f5bae21e645a6777f6950ea45163387f358dbc549e0efe82f592b6e5e229.jpg)

To be sure, even outside of vegetables, food prices were higher (0.9% m/m, sa) in June vis-à-vis the last few months, led by cereals and pulses. All this meant that food inflation at 8.4% oya in June was the main driver of headline inflation. However, cereals and pulses have not witnessed sharp increases in July, suggesting there are no generalized food price pressures as yet. Further, improving monsoon and sowing patterns augur well for future food price dynamics, as we recently pointed out. Consequently, food inflation is expected to soften once the vegetable shock fades (Figure 2).

Figure 2: CPI Food and Core inflation
![](images/690c18c5d81f25aab558a9d0f243e4caea89a18fde667ca857f0e534f013c02d.jpg)

On its part, the soft run of core-core prices continued, with core prices increasing by a benign 0.3% m/m, sa in June, like the readings in April and May. Core-core ex gold was even softer at 0.2% m/m, sa. On a year-on-year basis, core-core inflation remained unchanged at a series-low of 3.3%. That said, manufacturing PMI input and output prices have continued to rise in the last few months. Furthermore, we expect core-core prices to jump sharply in July and/or August, reflecting the one-time \~15% rise in mobile tariffs. Therefore core dynamics will need close watching.

All told, July inflation is currently tracking 3.5-3.7% oya despite a very favorable base effect. For 3Q, inflation is expected to be in the 4.1-4.2% range, higher than RBI’s forecast of 3.8%. However, the near-term jump in the inflation trajectory is expected to reverse by 4Q when we expect the vegetable price shock to reverse.

## May IP bucks the heat wave

May IP surprised to be upside printing at 5.9% oya (J.P. Morgan: 4.6%, Consensus: 4.8%). In sequential terms, IP came in at a strong 1.4% m/m, sa on the back of the 1.3% in April. While the anticipated strength of electricity and mining played out, manufacturing was expected to be impinged by the heatwave, but fortunately that does not seem to have played out.

On the contrary, the heat wave seems to have boosted consumer durables production which came in strong at 2.8% m/ m, sa in May on the back of 4.0% rise in April, despite softer auto production (Figure 3). That said, with auto inventories building up, there could be some payback in durables production in the coming months. Meanwhile, consumer non-durables – after a soft patch – rose sharply for the second month in a row (2.8% m/m, sa) which augurs well for rural consumption.

Figure 3: Consumer durable IP vs auto production %oya, both axes
![](images/2245518698d35bb2e218aaa72bc50526abe1816494733035ea619e87403314f1.jpg)

## July showers reduce rainfall deficit

After ending the month of June 11% below the Long Period Average (LPA), the cumulative rainfall deficit has reduced to 3% below LPA on the back of strong rainfall in recent days (Figure 4). Furthermore, spatial disparities have eased with South in a surplus of 6%, while north-west, east & north-east, and central India are in deficits of 1%, 3%, and 8% respectively.

Figure 4: Cumulative rainfall % deviation from LPA
![](images/1dd65f5599e1cdcc5c79d44a1e7e05fc6d4ecd3109de0e18e6f8821be7d68ef0.jpg)

Sowing has also picked up in a broad-based manner with the area under Kharif crops for the 2024-25 crop year higher by 14% vs last year. Area sown under rice – the major Kharif crop – increased by 19% after staying almost flat in June boosted by the pick-up in rainfall in July. The acreage of pulses and oilseeds has increased by 55% year-on-year. Government incentives through higher MSP increases of pulses, in particular Tur, and oilseeds have contributed to higher acreage of these crops. Furthermore, according to the Ministry of Agriculture's assessment with state governments, the area targeted for Kharif sowing of major vegetables, such as onion, tomato, and potato, has significantly increased vis-à-vis last year. Kharif onion and potato acreage is set to increase by 27% and 12% year-on-year respectively. However, targeted tomato acreage is only 2% higher vis-à-vis last year and tomato being a highly perishable vegetable is also impacted by heavy rainfall due to the related transport disruptions. This could continue to adversely impact its supply and keep tomato prices volatile in the foreseeable future.

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
